Pair any of these Viture glasses with the Mobile Dock Mini for Switch 2 compatibility, making them the only pair of smart glasses with official Switch 2 support.

Lastly is Viture Luma Ultra, down to $449 ($150 off). Luma Ultra sports the best image quality of all Viture glasses and includes myopia-adjusting diopters. These are the only pair that support 6DoF tracking when using the Viture Spacewalker app thanks to the cameras on the front, but there’s no native 3DoF support like Beast has, so this functionality is limited to content you view through the app.

#10416. Samsung just launched a gaming monitor with a refresh rate higher than its price — dual-mode Odyssey G60H offers 1,100 Hz in HD

The Odyssey G60H monitor breaks the 1 KHz barrier when flipped to HD mode.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Samsung's display business has produced some incredible screens, often with groundbreaking features. We have one such example released today: the Samsung Odyssey G60H (LS27HG602ENXZA), a 27" Fast IPS LCD gaming monitor whose standout feature is that it can refresh up to 1,100 times per second. That is 0.91 milliseconds per frame. This exotic screen isn't cheap, but if you're expecting a price to match the max refresh, be surprised, as you can snag it on Amazon for $894.

If you look at the specifications, you'll see Samsung describe it as a 600Hz gaming monitor. There's no overclocking shenanigans here; this is a "Dual Mode" monitor, meaning that in its native QHD resolution (2560×1440), it supports an already scorching 600Hz refresh rate. You have to slash the resolution by 75% to get that 1100Hz figure, down to just 1280×720, or "HD". Given that this is exactly 1/4 the resolution of QHD, Samsung has hopefully implemented some pretty fancy integer upscaling.

Aside from the resolution and refresh rate specs, this is actually a fairly standard IPS LCD gaming monitor. That's not a knock; it's perfectly fine, with a typical brightness of 400 cd/m², claimed 99% sRGB coverage, rated 1000:1 native contrast, and some measure of HDR10 support—although given the lack of local dimming and the modest brightness, it's hard to call this an HDR display. Naturally, it supports FreeSync Premium and is G-Sync Compatible, and while Samsung doesn't share the VRR ranges, it does say that you can't hit the peak 1,100 refresh rate with FreeSync enabled.

The firm does say that the G60H is the first to include "Odyssey Clear Motion," a feature that it says "reduces motion blur to help players see fast-moving action more clearly during critical moments in gameplay." It's not completely clear what this feature actually is, but Samsung says that "through the optimised blur reduction algorithm, the Odyssey G6 keeps fast-changing scenes clear and crisp."

Samsung notes that it plans to bring the technology to more gaming monitor models, which sounds interesting. Black frame insertion (BFI) and backlight strobing are the two most common methods of motion blur reduction, but Samsung has sold monitors with strobing for over ten years; it would be odd to reintroduce it as a new feature now.

There's an interesting question hanging over this feature: at 1,100Hz, does a monitor even need dedicated motion blur reduction? Blur Busters has long discussed 1,000Hz as something of a milestone for sample-and-hold displays, arguing that sufficiently high refresh rates can approach the motion clarity of a CRT without requiring backlight strobing.

1,000Hz isn't some magical threshold where motion blur disappears entirely, though. That site's own calculations still predict about one pixel of motion blur for every 1,000 pixels per second of movement at 1ms persistence, and that's assuming instantaneous pixel transitions (like an OLED). At 1,100Hz, that drops to about 0.91 pixels, which is certainly impressive, but not quite zero. The monitor's actual pixel response times also factor into the equation, of course, and Samsung's claimed 1ms GtG response time is actually a measured range of 1.00 to 1.99ms, rounded down to a whole number. I'd be shocked if the real pixel response times weren't more like 3-5ms in the worst case.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Whatever the case with the motion blur feature, in terms of connectivity, the Odyssey G6 can accept video through DisplayPort 2.1 or HDMI 2.1. It has two full-sized HDMI ports, and it also has a USB Type-B connector to pass through its pair of USB Type-A ports to the host machine. Curiously, no USB Type-C connectivity is to be found anywhere, which is unusual on a monitor of this price class, but given that this is clearly aimed at desktop gamers who have the hardware to push games to these kinds of frame rates (at least, with frame generation), it's a sensible omission to be sure, as is the elision of integrated speakers. There is a headphone jack to pass-through HDMI audio, though.

The other important characteristics you might like to know are that the Odyssey G6 (G60)'s included stand supports tilt, swivel, pivot, and height adjustments; it supports 100×100 VESA mounting, and that it uses an external AC adaptor, not an internal power supply, which is bad for convenience but good for repairability.

While the image quality is not going to blow you away if you're accustomed to OLEDs, having seen a 540-Hz display in person and been gobsmacked by it, 1,100 Hz sounds absolutely tantalizing. Admittedly, 1280x720 is a low resolution, but we used to play 240p console games on titanic projection TVs. Hardcore competitive gamers don't need high resolutions, so every cut to input lag helps.

#10413. Samsung might copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island soon, but it actually looks good [Video]

A new leak suggests that Samsung is preparing to copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island feature in the not-too-distant future, to an extent that not many other Android brands have even done thus far.

Copying the Dynamic Island was briefly like a rite of passage for some Android brands, with many wasting almost no time in delivering their own version of the feature. But, on many of those devices, it’s a surface-level ripoff at best. The feature is primarily limited to music apps and timers, rarely working with any third-party apps in meaningful ways. Android has a built-in feature similar to Apple’s but not in the same styling.

Samsung is now preparing its own version of the Dynamic Island, and it seems this one has a bit more meat on its bones.

SamMobile on Twitter/X posted a video and two still images of what appears to be a Dynamic Island clone coming to Galaxy devices. It’s mentioned as potentially coming to the Galaxy S27 series (so, One UI 9.5) and being called “Moment Effects.” A portion of the software explicitly mentions integration with Google Sports, the leak claims, while it seems Samsung is using Live Notifications here, which already support a number of third-party apps.

The current implementation of live activities showing the "chip" in the top bar is honestly far from good.

It negates one of the major pros of Android over iOS: being able to see notification icons in the top bar.

As when the "chip" of the live activity is active/shown, all notification icons will get hidden, even if there's a ton of space left between the "chip" and the constraint of the front camera area - an implementation that makes no sense whatsoever.

I honestly can't fathom how could Google come out with such poor implementation.

I don't particularly like this one from Samsung, but it surely is 1000000 times better from a UX perspective.

The kicker from a UX perspective is that Samsung’s interpretation allows you to swipe between activities, similar to Apple, but the core premise is similar with the island showing around the selfie camera cutout.

When this feature might surface does remain unclear, but a One UI 9.5 debut would make plenty of sense. That update is in beta now and due to launch on the Galaxy S27 series early next year.

What do you think of Samsung’s alleged Dynamic Island clone? Personally, I’m on board as long as it proves useful, and it seems to be on the right track compared to most other copycats.

The Nvidia board has reinstated Jensen Huang as the company’s CEO just 11 days after he resigned, a move that, although it had strong support from both employees and supporters, had surprised many. #10409. Galaxy Z Fold 8 features: What you can do on a foldable

Folding phones have come in numerous variations over the years as the concept has evolved. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is the latest evolution that introduces a form factor that fits every user. Its unique design makes it a foldable most people can actually use – which makes it a perfect gift. You might be surprised by what it can do.

It’s in the name. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 folds and unfolds like other foldable devices – that much you know. But what the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s form factor provides is something different to what most might be familiar with.

Instead of a tall device that unfolds into a near-uniform square display, you get a wider screen more like a tablet. That makes it perfect for watching videos or movies. Content that naturally works well on TVs or your computer will look great on the Galaxy Z Fold 8.

What about content that’s best viewed in portrait mode? Because it opens up to a tablet-like display, you can turn the Fold 8 to watch YouTube Shorts or Instagram Reels without a problem, so if you or someone you know likes to watch content on a bigger display, the Z Fold 8 makes that much easier. It really doesn’t matter what content you come across – the wider inner display gives you that large screen feeling.

Like a well-designed tool, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 has more purposes than the tasks best suited for the inner display. That shorter, wider profile makes the outer display smaller, but not as thin as something on other foldables, like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra.

In reality, the aspect ratio is similar enough across both the inner and outer screens that most content scales well on the shorter front panel. Social apps like X and Instagram look great because videos and images are able to fill the screen, rather than squeeze. Samsung’s One UI software also supports app optimization across both screens, although the experience may vary by app.

Saying the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s outer display is “minimal” isn’t a dig, either. Apps can be refreshing to use when you don’t need to open them on a large scale. Take Messages, for instance. It’s not a complex app, and there isn’t a ton to see – just the last couple of messages exchanged. The same goes for plenty of apps available on the Google Play Store.

And guess what? If you don’t like using the app on a smaller display, just unfold the phone. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is such a versatile phone, whether it’s a gift during the holidays or one for yourself.

There are instances where you need the large real estate. One UI 9 gives the Galaxy Z Fold 8 the option to open and run multiple apps on your screen. There are several ways you can do it, too.

The main approach is to split two apps on the wide inner display. But if you want to see more from one app, you can simply drag the split over in one direction. One UI 9 leaves room to add one more app to that split screen, giving you a three-way Split View. All it takes is dragging an app from the taskbar and choosing the spot.

If you truly need a fourth app open, you have the option to open another in a floating window. Having four apps open at once is definitely a niche instance, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8 handles it like it’s nothing. You can have YouTube up in one corner while you check Messages for gift ideas and shop through the Amazon app. It might be helpful to keep a list of what you got and for whom in a floating Sheets window.

The point is not only that it’s technically possible, but it’s usable. So often devices let users open apps in split screen, but are in no way comfortable enough for the task. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is perfect for it, and the experience actually makes you more productive, not less.

Most phones don’t let you get the best angle without any sort of third-party accessory. The Galaxy Z Fold 8, though, acts like its own tripod in a way.

Let’s say you’re taking photos, and you need to get a shot of yourself, but no one is around to take the photo for you. The Galaxy Z Fold 8’s hinge lets it prop itself up, so you can do things like set a timer and run to strike a pose.

This comes in handy for videos, too. Think of how many times you wanted to get a good angle on a plane or somewhere where there’s this flat surface just asking for a tiny tripod or kickstand. Well, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is its own kickstand and can be positioned at just the right angle.

You can get the Galaxy Z Fold 8 from your favorite carriers and retailers, but Best Buy is one of the leading places to find deals during the holiday season. You’ll find hefty discounts, higher trade-in values for your old devices, and more.

#10408. Gemini app rolls out very slick camera redesign on Android

Google has thoroughly redesigned Gemini for Android’s in-app camera that lets you quickly add an image to prompts.

Available from the ‘plus’ menu in the prompt box, the old interface launched a fullscreen viewfinder that was quite generic. This UI is still in use when you launch the camera from the Gemini overlay.

The new one appears inline on the Gemini homepage or inside a conversation. It launches as a bottom sheet that you can drag up — with a really slick animation — to take up most of the screen.

This nearly edge-to-edge redesign, which extends behind the system navigation bar, is a stark contrast to the old approach.

To the right of the capture button is a three-dot overflow button that animates on open, revealing options to control the flash and switch to the front-facing camera. There’s a back button on the other side, but you can also just swipe down.

This camera redesign first launched in the Gemini for iOS app, but has now widely rolled out on Android in both the beta and stable (version 17.76+) channels. Be sure to Force stop the Google app if you’re not seeing it live yet.

This follows the launch of GIF creation in the Gemini app yesterday. Powered by Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash, select GIF from the Format menu or specify “Create a GIF” in your prompt. Google frames this feature as an alternative to searching for GIFs.

#10407. How to Test WCAG 2.2 in CI: What axe-core Misses

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Your accessibility check in CI is green. axe-core ran, zero violations, and the badge in the README looks great. Your site can still fail WCAG 2.2.

That isn't a bug in axe. WCAG 2.2 became a W3C Recommendation in October 2023. It added nine success criteria and dropped one (4.1.1 Parsing), and most of the new ones can't be judged by reading the DOM. Deque said as much when it shipped the first WCAG 2.2 support in axe-core 4.5: the target-size rule would probably be the only new-criterion rule they added, because the others can't be automated without a lot of false positives.

So the rest is on you. This article covers the one criterion axe can check and three it can't (focus not obscured, dragging movements, accessible authentication), with Playwright tests you can drop into a project. I ran all of them against a small test page that fails every criterion on purpose, and against a fixed version of the same page. If you want a plain-language rundown of every change first, WCAG 2.2 vs 2.1 goes through them. Here I only care about what you can put in a pipeline.

Level A and AA are what most contracts and laws point at, so those are the ones that matter first. The AAA criteria are still worth knowing about.

That's four AA criteria with something you can script, one of which axe already handles. The rest of this article goes through those four.

The AA rule is smaller than most people expect: a pointer target should be at least 24 by 24 CSS pixels, or sit far enough from its neighbours that a 24px circle centred on it wouldn't overlap another target or that target's own circle. There are exceptions, including inline links in a sentence, and targets whose size the browser controls.

axe-core has a target-size rule for this. One catch: it isn't part of a default run. In my setup (axe-core 4.14.1 through @axe-core/playwright 4.14.0), a plain analyze() on the broken page reported nothing at all. The rule only showed up once I asked for it:

On the broken page this lists the three 16px toolbar buttons by selector. On the fixed page, with 32px buttons, it passes. If you've been running axe for years and never seen a target-size result, this is why.

Some context on how common the problem is. In the scan data behind my last SitePoint article, 34.5% of the 1,025 sites had controls below a stricter 44px threshold. That is a tougher standard than the 24px that AA asks for, so don't read it as "a third of sites fail 2.5.8". Plenty of those would pass. But it does show how many production sites ship tiny controls.

If you'd like a quick look at one page before wiring anything up, browser tools such as axe DevTools, WAVE, or a WCAG checker will show the same thing.

axe can't test it, because you have to press Tab and look at where the browser scrolled. Two details caught me out building this:

The check below samples a grid of points inside the visible part of the focused element and asks the browser what's on top at each one. If none of the points land on the element or its children, it's covered.

On my broken page it reported nine hits: links hidden behind the footer going forward ("Section 4 link", "Section 10 link", "Section 16 link") and the toolbar buttons and a few links hidden behind the header coming back. The output names the element and the key, which is what you want when you're staring at a failed build.

The fix is one CSS rule, sized to your sticky elements:

That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. With it in place the same test passes.

Be aware of what this can't see: a modal that opens over the page, or a chat widget that only appears after a delay, will not show up unless your test opens or waits for them. Adrian Roselli's piece on 2.4.11 and adversarial conformance is worth reading for the awkward cases.

Anything you can do by dragging needs a way to do it with a single click or tap, unless dragging is essential. Sortable lists, sliders, kanban cards, file reorderers: all of them.

You can't prove a drag alternative works from a script. But you can catch the obvious mistake, which is a draggable item with no button anywhere near it:

The limit is real. It only recognises the native draggable attribute. Libraries built on pointer events, such as dnd-kit, don't set it, so this test will walk right past them. If you use one of those, replace the selector with whatever your component renders, or add a test per drag component. Treat it as a tripwire for the common case, not a verdict.

The fix, in the fixed page, is a pair of "Move up" and "Move down" buttons on each item, each with an accessible name that says which item it moves.

You can't make someone solve a cognitive test to log in (remember a password, transcribe a code) unless there's an alternative or a mechanism that helps. Password managers count as that mechanism, which means the two ways sites break it are blocking paste and omitting the right autocomplete values.

This dispatches a synthetic paste event and checks whether anything called preventDefault() on it. It catches the old onpaste="return false" trick, and it catches script-based blockers too. It does not tell you whether your login has a CAPTCHA that needs an alternative, or an emailed one-time code you must retype. Those need a person.

On a pull request, a plain GitHub Actions job is enough:

Point PAGE_URL at a running preview, and run the tests against the pages that matter most: sign-in, checkout, anything with a sticky element. Don't try to crawl everything on the first pass. A test that fails for reasons nobody owns gets deleted within a month.

Four things I haven't tried to automate, and wouldn't:

Automation will keep the regressions out. It won't tell you the work is finished. A green build and a conformant site are different things, and WCAG 2.2 widened the gap between them.

The tests and both test pages are small enough to read in one sitting. Copy what's useful, change the selectors, and keep the broken page around: it's a handy way to check that a test fails when it should.

About the author: [name] builds WCAGsafe, an accessibility scanner. The study data above comes from its scans; the code in this article is independent of it and runs against any page.

#10406. Bambu Lab P3C Review: A more affordable tool changer

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Bambu Lab P3C is the lower-cost little brother of the H3C. It has the same type of tools and outstanding TPU printing process as its larger sibling, but drops the heated chamber, filament dryer, and automatic filament loading. If you want an upgradable tool changer on a more manageable budget, the P3C is a great place to start.

Works with multiple materials and nozzle sizes in the same print

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Bambu Lab didn’t even tease the arrival of the new P3C, a smaller, more budget-friendly version of the H3C, which retails at roughly half the price. In fact, this post might be the first time you’ve heard more than just a rumor about the tool changer’s mysterious little brother.

The Bambu Lab P3C combo is a tool changer with room for seven tools and a 256 x 256 x 260mm build volume, roughly the same as the P2S. It ships as either a five-tool unit, with two empty slots for later customization, or as a seven-tool “multi-material combo” with six 1.75mm tools and one 2.85mm tool. Bambu is bringing back old school 2.85mm filament in the form of super squishy TPU 75A.

The five-tool unit is priced at $1,149, while the seven-tool is launching at $1,299.

It offers the same excellent TPU printing process for both standard and phat TPU and has quickly joined the select group of send it and forget it printers in my fleet. I’ve had very few failures, even when printing more technical filaments.

The P3C is not quite as tall as the H3C, but it does require almost 4ft (1,212 mm) of vertical space for the AMS lid to fully open. So plan accordingly and have a good space picked out in advance.

Bambu’s most annoying cost-saving measure took the A right out of AMS. There’s no motor of any sort in the AMS 3P, leaving you to manually load and unload the filament all the way to the nozzle. The process takes a long time if you are loading all of the filament slots. The upside of not having powered rollers is that the AMS 3P will run almost any 1kg or smaller spool without issue.

The printer can run different-sized nozzles from .2 mm to .8 mm, and has an optional 2.85 mm TPU filament tool. The FlexFeed extruder with Active Grip Control just pushes whatever filament it’s asked to while changing on the fly. It may be the best extruder I’ve reviewed, however, the printer as a whole is not quite what we need for the best 3D printers of 2026.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

256 x 256 x 260 mm (10.07 x 10.07 x 10.23 inches)

330 x 320 x 325 mm (12.99 x 12.59 x 12.79 inches)

PLA/TPU/ABS/ASA/PA/PC/PET/Fiber reinforced

PLA/TPU/ABS/ASA/PA/PC/PET/PPS/Fiber reinforced (up to 350 degrees)

PEI textured spring steel sheet (110 degrees)

PEI textured spring steel sheet (120 degrees)

413 x 538 x 925 mm (16.25 x 21.18 x 36.4 in)

493 x 571 x 1016 mm (19.40 x 22.48 x 40 inches)

The Bambu Lab P3C comes with five .4mm tools preinstalled in the bottom of the AMS 3P. Also included are a toolkit and cables needed to assemble the printer, along with quick start guides for both the printer and AMS 3P, and a few spare parts. It does not come with a USB memory stick, which is needed for recording time lapses.

We received the multi-material combo for review, which came with five tools installed, plus two additional tools I had to set up myself (one 1.75mm and one 2.85mm), along with several spools of PLA Palette and 2.85mm TPU 75A.

The P3C is missing several perks of the bigger H3C in order to save costs and keep the price reasonable. The most disappointing is the lack of a motor to drive the filament spools. This makes the “A” in the AMS 3P questionable, as you are forced to manually feed the filament all the way to the nozzle, as well as handle retraction when unloading.

The AMS 3P also does not come with a filament drier, although an optional 75C dryer will be available for $159.90.

The basic Bambu Lab P3C comes standard with only five tools and is expandable to seven. The Swaptek system keeps the cooling fans and extruder on the toolhead, with each tool having its own filament path, heater, and a hardened steel nozzle. When printing, the idle tools are preheated, allowing for extremely fast tool swaps.

The nozzles on each tool can be swapped out with different sizes, and multiple sizes can be used in the same print.

Bambu Lab will also release tools for cutting, drawing, debossing, and foil transfer in the future.

The Bambu Lab P3C supports printing TPU with a bypass port on all filament slots except 2 and 6, which allows TPU to skip the tube, reducing friction. If you have the optional 2.85mm tool, you can mount it to any slot with a bypass.

Please note that the 2.85mm tool has a wider filament path – it's not just the nozzle tip that changes dimensions.

The tools dock on a rack in front of the printer which retracts after each tool change, allowing for a clear view of the build plate.

The Flexfeed extruder is impressive. It switches from 1.75mm to 2.85mm filament on the fly and uses what Bambu Lab calls Active Grip Control with feedback from the extruder motor.

Like the H3C, there are magnetic covers on the left and right sides of the AMS and a front cover that attaches in slots to allow easy access to the chamber for maintenance. The front cover is a bit tricky, and you need to lift the AMS up to get it free. For this reason, I didn’t bother to secure the AMS to the base of the printer. You really need to remove that front panel to change out the tools.

Changing tools is one of the few frustrating things about the Bambu Lab P3C (or the H3C, as it’s the same process). There is very little room to work, and there are sharp plastic edges that left me bloodied on one occasion. I would suggest picking your favorite configuration and leaving it there if you can.

The AMS is massive and requires a lot of additional vertical space to fully open the lid to get at the spools. Bambu Lab recommends a clearance of 1,212 mm or almost four feet from the base in order to open the lid. Surprisingly, this smaller P3C is only 3.58 inches shorter than the big H3C. With the printer sitting on a standard countertop, a step-stool will be mandatory equipment for most people. Bambu offers a rolling cart for the H3C that puts the printer at a more manageable height, and I would highly recommend getting it for the P3C.

The base unit of the Bambu Lab P3C looks similar to the P and X series printers, with a plastic outer frame and a steel chassis. The build plate is a standard 256 x 256 mm, with 260 mm available on the Z-axis. The P3C does not have active chamber heating, but being enclosed with a max of 300C on the nozzle and 110C on the bed, it has the ability to print many of the more common technical filaments. The chamber is ventilated with HEPA and active carbon filtration.

The 3D printer has a great camera, which is used for AI detection and monitoring using the Bambu Handy app and the device page of Bambu Slicer. The camera can produce time-lapses of your prints but does require a USB memory stick to store them.

The Bambu Lab P3C and AMS 3P ship in separate boxes but are almost fully assembled. After removing the packaging, there are two screws holding the dock retainers that need to be removed and 6 screws securing the bed in place.

There is a helpful reminder to do this when the printer is first turned on. AMS 3P is mounted on top of the printer and requires a good deal of vertical space. There are four screws to attach the AMS 3P to the printer, but I left these off since the front panel of the AMS is easier to remove when you can tip the AMS back a little.

The individual tools attach to the dock magnetically and must be placed manually, which can take a little fiddling to get used to.

The touch screen attaches to the front of the printer without tools, and the ribbon cable to connect it is just long enough to work.

The Bambu Lab P3C levels its bed as part of its initial calibration and again at the start of every print. The system works perfectly.

The Bambu Lab P3C does not have auto-loading, as there is no motor.

When loading multiple new filaments for a print, the process can take a while and is very hands-on. When using normal 1.75 mm filament, the material is pushed into the orange tube in the AMS when called for by the printer.

When using TPU, either 1.75mm or 2.85 filament, drop the filament into a slot with a bypass port in the bottom and drag it by hand to the tool. The normal Bowden tube must be removed from the tool and clipped to the side. When running 1.75mm TPU a short piece of bowden tube must be inserted into the tool to help feed the filament correctly. The 2.85mm TPU doesn’t need the short tube and can feed directly.

The printer has a helpful filament guide to walk you through the loading operation.

Bambu Lab-branded filament with RFID tags will automatically enter the filament type and color. Other brands must be entered manually via the printer’s touchscreen.

Unloading filament is the reverse of loading. You’ll need to manually retract material once it is cut by the printer. There is a batch unloading command that will allow you to wait until all of the selected filaments have been cut.

Bambu Studio is based on PrusaSlicer and its predecessors. New for the P3C and the H3C is the ability to mix different tool sizes. It is one of the best slicers available with good filament mapping, control and print monitoring, with access to Makerworld, Bambu Lab’s file sharing service.

The P3C does not come with filament samples. Bambu Lab sent a few spools of their new PLA Palette and 2.85mm 75A TPU to try. Be sure to check out our best filaments for 3D printing to fill up your AMS 3P.

This print of Carl and Donut from the Dungeon Crawler Carl book series took 15 hours and 35 minutes to print using a .16mm layer height for smooth detail. I used the default settings, but slowed the outer walls to 65mm/s. The print is exceptionally good, though the supports left a few marks under horizontal areas like the arms and shin. Normally I’d tilt a model this back 45 degrees to save time and support, but the fin on this particular model is vexing and would have added an hour to the print time and another 10g of support.

This is printed in seven colors of PLA, maxing out the P3C slots. Polymaker HT-PLA Pro White, Prusament Galaxy Black and Galaxy Silver PLA, Elegoo PLA Brown and Silk Red, Inland Bone White, and Matterhacker’s Purple MH Build Series PLA.

Printing with seven colors allows you more creative freedom when printing complex models. Though the brown did a lot of heavy lifting on this print, I think the P3C really captured Carl nicely.

I tried some of Bambu Lab’s matte PETG on a Cute Blue Jay from Makerworld. Since the model prints best straight up and down, and requires pretty extensive supports, I used a scrap of Prusa Lipstick Red PLA as an interface layer.

Using a .16mm layer height, the print took 8 hours and 55 minutes running at a little under 100mm/s on average. The matte PETG makes the model look like it was painted. This is printed in black, white and baby blue matte PETG and the supports came off with no residue what so ever.

Though the P3C doesn’t get hot enough to print PPS, it does print most PA, PC, and PET filaments very well. I printed a spool and a half of Sunlu Easy PA, making all of the mechanical parts for two radio-controlled Death Racers. Initially, I made just a few items per plate, but with just a little glue stick, the bed adhesion was so good that I felt comfortable loading up the plates as full as possible and had zero failures. All were printed with a .2mm layer height and slowed to 100mm/s by me in the slicer. Other than a tiny bit of stringing, the parts are perfect mechanically.

The Bambu Lab P3C’s HEPA filter does a good job filtering the stinkier filaments like ABS and ASA. I printed this wire holding vice using Fusion ABS matte ionized cobalt black and Prusa lipstick red ASA.

This is printed with a .2 mm layer height and an average print speed of 120mm/s. The entire print took a little less than 2 hours and 3 minutes, and the parts fit together perfectly.

With the H3C and now the P3C, I have seen the future of printing with TPU. The AMS 3P bypass ports are just the ticket for printing soft materials.

I printed several plates of treads for my RC Death Racer project. Normally, I print these a few at a time to limit the fallout from print failures, but this proved unnecessary. I ran 48 treads in Bambu Lab Cyan 2.85mm TPU 75A and another 48 using Bambu Lab 90A TPU. Both prints used a .2mm layer height and were limited to 100 mm/s in the slicer. The 75A print finished a little quicker at 19 hours 10 minutes, with the 90A filament taking 19 hours and 40 minutes.

Both are terrific prints with no stringing at all and zero failures.

The P3C can print almost any filament I own beautifully. It lacks a few creature comforts like auto-loading, filament drying (which can be upgraded), and a heated chamber. It’s also tall enough to require a step stool to load those back filament slots.

The Bambu Lab P3C is best for anyone who wants poop-free multicolor, multi-material printing without paying H3C money. I highly recommend it for anyone who plans to print a lot of TPU and doesn’t need that larger build plate. If you can live without the automatic feeding you normally get from a Bambu printer, then the money you’ll save in filament is worth the hassle.

This machine gives you most of the advantages of the Bambu Lab H3C at roughly half the $2,499 price tag. The open-source four-color Snapmaker U1 is still worth it right now while it’s on sale for $699. Another option is the Creality K3, which is another four-color tool changer with a similar build size for $849.

Denise Bertacchi is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware US, covering 3D printing. Denise has been crafting with PCs since she discovered Print Shop had clip art on her Apple IIe. She loves reviewing 3D printers because she can mix all her passions: printing, photography, and writing.

#10404. The Google Home app is better than Nest alone ever was, mostly

The Nest app is officially dead, but it’s worth taking a step back and remembering that, while there are some rough edges, the Google Home app is a better smart home experience than Nest alone ever could have been.

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

The Nest app has been on life support for years now. Google has been migrating legacy products into the Home app for a while and the last few puzzle pieces came in Nest Protect support, the death of the earliest Nest Thermostats, and other assorted adjustments.

Realistically, even long-time Nest users have had no reason to continue using the app over Google Home.

Having been personally, deeply invested in Google’s vision for the smart home over the past decade, I’ll say that the Google Home app we have today is better than the Nest app ever was or ever could be. A huge part of that comes from Google Home’s ability to pull together devices from third-party ecosystems. Being able to control your entire smart home from a single app is how the smart home always should have been, and between Google’s work and the Matter standard, we’re finally able to enjoy that pretty seamlessly for the most part. Automations, Google’s actually-useful AI features, and more only sweeten the deal. I’m also a big fan of what the Home app has become from a UX perspective. While there are always rough edges and bugs to clean up, the customizable homepage and well-organized sections make for a good – not perfect, but good – experience for the end user.

That said, there is one thing that the Nest app’s limited scope benefitted from, and that’s a focus on making your own product great.

Google’s move to the Home app for Nest devices has been a 5-year run of trying to clean things up, improve on the app, and basically bring back the way things were in the Nest app as far as the first-party cameras and thermostats are concerned. It’s taken ages, but we’re finally up to speed, mostly. I still hate that you have to reset Nest Cams to change Wi-Fi when the Nest app just let you change it, and the Nest app’s settings were much less overwhelming, but I’d say the pros far outweigh the cons at this point.

The Nest app was good at its job, but the Google Home app is a worthy successor, finally, and now the Nest app can finally rest. I mostly think the same applies to Gemini vs Google Assistant, with the newer assistant a better overall experience, but it’s easier to poke holes in that argument.

Disagree? What do you think of the Google Home app, and were you still using the Nest app?

#10402. Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 vs Watch 9: When is it right to spend more?

The Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 is worth spending more on if you want longer battery life, a brighter outdoor display, and specialized features for trail running and diving. The Galaxy Watch 9 is the better fit if you want Samsung’s latest everyday health tools in a smaller, lighter watch.

Both, however, are fantastic smartwatches with limitless potential. They share a Snapdragon Wear Elite processor and many of Samsung’s newest wellness features, so deciding between them is really about figuring out your priorities when it comes to buying a wearable this holiday season. We’re here to help you figure that out.

As we covered in our Galaxy Watch 9 and Watch Ultra 2 comparison, both watches move to Qualcomm’s Snapdragon Wear Elite platform. Both also ship with Wear OS 7 and One UI 8 Watch.

That shared foundation includes useful health insights. Vitals brings overnight measurements together to establish your personal baseline, while Daily Cardio Load helps you understand training demands and recovery. Hearing monitoring can flag potentially unsafe noise levels, whether you’re at a concert or somewhere unexpectedly loud.

Both also get five years of Wear OS updates and security patches, making either a useful long-term partner for your Samsung device, like the Galaxy S26 Ultra or Galaxy Z Fold8.

The clearest differences are size, battery capacity, display brightness, and outdoor capabilities. Here’s how the lineup breaks down:

As you can tell, the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 is considerably bigger and heavier, but you get a lot more capability for that additional size.

The Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 makes its strongest case when your plans regularly take you beyond a desk, a gym, or a charger.

Samsung rates the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 for up to 60 hours with the always-on display enabled, compared with up to 30 hours for the Watch 9, and that extra headroom is appealing when your day starts with a workout and finishes well into the evening.

Then there’s the screen. In our hands-on with both watches, the Ultra 2’s brightness stood out immediately, with a higher peak that gives it an advantage when checking workout information outdoors, even in the bright sun. And its titanium frame provides added durability.

The Ultra 2 also adds Trail Run tracking for elevation and terrain impact, route guidance using compatible GPX files, and Nutrition Alert hydration guidance. These are tangible reasons to upgrade if your usual run involves trails instead of pavement.

Diving is another differentiator. Samsung specifies recreational diving support to depths of up to 40 meters, with automatic tracking of depth, dive time and water temperature — a remarkable feat for a piece of technology that lives on your wrist.

The best choice becomes clearer when you picture your own routine:

Choose the Galaxy Watch 9 for everyday wellness, a lighter fit, and strong value. Choose the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 for longer adventures, more demanding outdoor activities, and greater freedom between charges.

If you’re replacing an older Galaxy Watch, both offer a compelling new generation of health insights and long-term support. Both last a long time and look absolutely awesome with running shorts or a business suit. But the Watch Ultra 2 can take you that extra mile, literally and figuratively, and may be worth spending more on during Best Buy’s Holiday Deals event.

#10397. Which Samsung folding phone is right for you? Galaxy Z Fold 8, Ultra, or Flip 8

Folding phones are entering a new chapter and, if you’re looking to try one for the first time, you might not know exactly where to start. In this guide, we’ll take a look at some of the most important questions to answer when buying a foldable, and dissect how Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8, Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra, and Galaxy Z Flip 8 fit the bill to figure out which is the best for you, especially if you’re interested in one this holiday season.

Samsung has three foldables in its lineup this year, two of which are pretty familiar. The Galaxy Z Flip 8 continues the lineup of clamshell-style foldables, offering a form factor similar to that of a classic flip phone. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra, meanwhile, is designed to be a normal smartphone on the outside, hiding a tablet within. Finally, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is the new kid on the block, offering a compact outer display that opens up to a media-friendly, wider inner panel.

Each one serves a different need, but they all share similar performance and core specs. Here’s a quick rundown of the key details on each device:

Starting with the displays, the book-style Galaxy Z Fold 8 and Fold 8 Ultra both provide outer displays that are fully usable as standard smartphones, while the inner display is useful for multitasking, media, or just getting more space for reading a website or e-book. Samsung’s top-tier specs deliver performance for everything you’d need, with Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 for Galaxy powering everything from demanding games or media-editing apps to day-to-day use.

With each Samsung foldable, you’re getting a fully capable smartphone, but finding the one that’s best for you is a matter of picking your most important priorities. Cameras, battery life, content consumption, and size are some of the most important points to consider, so let’s take a look one-by-one.

When it comes to cameras, foldables have a tough time. There’s only so much physical space to fit the camera sensors and, with cameras, having more space generally results in a battery overall camera.

That said, the best camera on a Samsung foldable is undoubtedly on the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra.

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra has a 200MP main camera that’s similar to the Galaxy S26 Ultra, while it also packs a 50MP ultrawide and something neither of the others offer, a telephoto lens. The 10MP telephoto shooter captures faraway subjects at 3x optical zoom, meaning you’re not giving up on quality when you zoom in. Two 10MP cameras can be found on the displays, with neither sacrificing quality for an under-display look.

Runner-up here is the Galaxy Z Fold 8, which offers a pair of 50MP cameras for wide and ultrawide shots. You can get 2x optical-quality zoom out of the main sensor to still get a good zoom shot, while the main camera still offers good quality and Samsung’s best camera features including 2x optical-quality zoom and the subject-tracking My FanCam.

The Galaxy Z Flip 8 may not have the best camera hardware, but what it lacks in hardware it makes up for in flexibility. This is definitely the best form factor of the bunch for using the foldable design as a tripod for capturing clips. All three devices also use their foldable nature to let you capture full-quality selfies using the rear cameras, which always have better quality than the 10MP dedicated front-facing cameras.

Here’s where specs on paper can tell a different story compared to real life.

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra may offer the biggest battery of the bunch at 5,000 mAh, but its battery life is on par with the smaller Galaxy Z Fold 8. Both foldables are designed to last you through a full day, though sticking to the outer display only on the Fold 8 Ultra may bring you slightly better battery life overall.

In either case, you’re also getting 45W fast charging over USB-C, granting you speedy recharging if your device happens to get low through the course of a busy day. These are also “Qi2 Ready,” meaning you can use magnetic wireless chargers if you use a compatible magnetic case.

One of the most important things you’ll want to ask yourself when buying a foldable phone is why you want to opt for a foldable in the first place, and each of these devices gives a different answer.

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra is a productivity powerhouse, delivering more space on its inner display for you to get work done through the web, apps, and more. You can run up to three apps at once on the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra’s display, as well as floating apps, and more.

Galaxy Z Fold 8 is great for media consumption. The wider aspect ratio is perfect for watching videos, playing games, reading books, and more, all while still giving you plenty of space for productivity just like the Ultra.

Galaxy Z Flip 8, meanwhile, is all about giving you a more compact device. The outer display lets you quickly reply to messages or keep up with directions, while you can unfold it to do everything you want to on a normal phone. Close it up to more easily stow it away in your pocket or your bag.

If you are moving into a new Galaxy foldable, the good news is that you don’t have to start from scratch. Thanks to Samsung’s Smart Switch app, you can easily transfer supported content including pictures, contacts, messages, and more from an older Galaxy phone, another Android phone, or even an iPhone over to a Galaxy foldable. Apps, messages, photos, and more can all make the move wirelessly, without the need of a cable.

You can get the Galaxy Z Fold 8, Fold 8 Ultra, and Flip 8 from your favorite carriers and retailers, but Best Buy is one of the best places to score a great deal with solid trade-in values and deep discounts around Black Friday and throughout the holiday season.

Sivilya brings deep cybersecurity and technology leadership to Checkmarx as AI transforms software development.

MOUNTAIN VIEW, CA — November 5, 2026 — Checkmarx, the leader in agentic application security, today announced the appointment of Kobi Sivilya as Chief Information Security Officer (CISO) and Chief Information Officer (CIO). In this dual role, Sivilya will oversee the company’s security, IT and business applications, supporting Checkmarx’s global operations and continued growth.

Sivilya will lead Checkmarx’s corporate security and IT functions, aligning security, technology and business applications with the company’s strategic priorities. He will also help advance Checkmarx’s mission to secure modern application development.

#10395. Hostinger VPS in 2026: Hands-On With the AI-Managed VPS

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, renting a VPS meant signing up to be a part-time sysadmin. You’d SSH into a blank Ubuntu prompt and lose a Saturday to Nginx configs and firewall rules before writing a single line of your actual project.

In 2026, three things have changed. What we build has changed: AI coding tools turn weekend ideas into working apps, and AI agents need a machine that stays on after your laptop closes. Who runs servers has changed: you can now ask an AI agent in your terminal to set up a reverse proxy, close a port or work out why something broke. And what servers cost has changed: the AI boom is soaking up the world’s memory supply, and hosting prices are rising with it.

So I bought the smallest Hostinger VPS plan, deployed a Node.js app in Docker, and handed the DevOps work to Hostinger Agent, the AI assistant built into the dashboard.

Below is what that felt like step by step and whether it’s worth locking in a price before they climb further.

Most VPS hosting for developers now falls into four buckets. They all run on the same kind of machine; what changes is how much RAM you give them.

Apps built with AI coding tools. Claude Code and Cursor get you to a working Node.js or Python app fast, then leave you wondering where to put it. A VPS gives you one fixed monthly bill instead of usage-based pricing. If you want push-to-deploy, Hostinger offers Ubuntu templates with Coolify or Dokploy preinstalled.

AI agents and automations that run 24/7. For most people, a “VPS for AI” means a CPU box that never sleeps, not a GPU. Think n8n workflows, a scheduled scraper, or your own agent in a Docker container. Hostinger even has an “Ubuntu 24.04 with Claude Code” template: SSH in, run claude /login, and the agent keeps working after you close your laptop.

APIs, backends and databases for side projects. A Postgres instance, a Redis cache and a small API behind a reverse proxy fit comfortably on a mid-sized plan. You pick the versions, the extensions and the backup schedule.

Client sites and stores. Freelancers outgrow shared hosting when one client needs a custom PHP extension or a WooCommerce store starts to crawl. A VPS with cPanel or Plesk, or a WordPress or WooCommerce template, keeps client work isolated and under your control.

If you’d rather not build anything from scratch, the Docker Manager catalog listed 1,017 one-click templates when I checked, including 93 under AI/ML and 162 under Developer tools.

For this Hostinger VPS review, the question I cared about was simple: how long from “Choose plan” to an app on the internet? On KVM 1, the entry plan, it was about 12 minutes. Here’s each step with rough timings.

I went with KVM 1: 1 vCPU, 4 GB RAM, 50 GB NVMe storage and 4 TB of bandwidth. On the Black Friday deal it costs $6.49/mo, paid upfront as $155.76 for 24 months, and renews at $11.99/mo.

You choose the server’s location and what to install during checkout, alongside your billing details. The location picker shows estimated latency for each data centre; mine showed about 80 ms.

Install options come in three tabs: Plain OS, Control panel and Applications. I picked plain Ubuntu 26.04 LTS to see how much work the AI would take off my hands. A daily backup add-on is offered, but weekly backups are already included.

The five-minute estimate was about right. The “you’re ready” screen then gives you two ways in: the VPS dashboard, or a pre-filled ssh root@ command. It also offers a free domain for a year to use as the server’s hostname.

The overview shows the OS, plan, CPU, RAM, disk and bandwidth at a glance, with Docker Manager, Backups & Monitoring, Security and DNS Manager in the side menu.

Every server also gets a hostname under hstgr.cloud (mine was srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud), which came in handy later for SSL. I’ve masked my hostname as srvXXXXXXX throughout and blurred IP addresses in the screenshots.

Plain Ubuntu doesn’t ship with Docker. Open Docker Manager in hPanel and it installs Docker for you with one click. From there you can deploy apps from a Compose file, a repository URL or the one-click catalog.

Click Web console and you’re logged in as root in a browser tab, with Hostinger Agent in a panel beside it. You don’t need an SSH client or keys. The login banner flagged 33 pending updates, 23 of them security fixes; more on that later. A quick docker --version confirmed Docker 29.9.3 was ready.

Here’s the whole app: a tiny HTTP server with a health check, and a Dockerfile on Node.js 24, a supported LTS line.

The build took 8.3 seconds, including pulling node:24-alpine, and the health check answered straight away. The --restart unless-stopped flag brings the container back after a crash or reboot, which I tested later.

That’s a working app, but on plain HTTP, on a high port, with no TLS. Getting it production-ready is the DevOps part I handed to the AI.

An AI-managed VPS isn’t the same as a traditional managed VPS. You still get a self-managed server with full root access, but Hostinger Agent does the work you’d normally do yourself. Agent (previously called Kodee) is an MCP-powered assistant that lives in the dashboard and beside the AI web terminal, and it’s included at no extra cost. You can also connect Claude or Cursor to your server through Hostinger’s MCP server.

My prompt: “Check that Docker is installed, then set up a reverse proxy with a free SSL certificate so my demo-app container (port 3000) is served at https://srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud.”

It started by looking around: the Docker and Compose versions, which ports were in use, whether a web server was already running, and the OS release. Then it installed Caddy from Caddy’s official repository, configured the proxy and made sure ufw allowed web traffic.

It read Caddy’s logs to confirm the Let’s Encrypt certificate, which arrived in about 6 seconds. Finally it tested its own work: HTTPS returned 200, and plain HTTP returned a 308 redirect. The whole task took about two minutes.

To give it a real fault, I ran docker stop demo-app and reloaded the site. Then I asked why it was returning a 502 and to fix it.

In a single command, it checked the container state (Exited (137)) and searched Caddy’s logs, where it found connect: connection refused on port 3000. It restarted the container, confirmed /health and HTTPS were back, and even pointed to my docker stop in the terminal history. It also explained that --restart unless-stopped covers crashes and reboots, but not a manual stop.

A tip it didn’t mention: exit code 137 means Docker had to force-kill the container, because Node running as PID 1 ignores SIGTERM. Add --init to docker run for clean, instant shutdowns.

This was the most impressive answer. ufw already allowed only those three ports, but Agent noticed port 3000 was still reachable from the internet, because Docker-published ports bypass ufw rules.

It recreated the container bound to 127.0.0.1:3000, so the app is only reachable through Caddy, and confirmed with an external port check. It left DNS, time sync and Caddy’s local admin port running, explaining that stopping them would break DNS and time sync.

Then I asked it to reboot the server. First it checked that Docker and Caddy start on boot and that the container’s restart policy was set, then it rebooted. The site came back over HTTPS without any help from me.

Nothing I asked for was destructive, so Agent just got on with it: the firewall change, the container rebuild and the reboot all ran without a confirmation prompt.

The Agent panel says so upfront, noting that terminal commands can run without one. For destructive actions such as reinstalling the OS or restoring a backup, Hostinger’s FAQ says Agent always asks for confirmation first.

One thing worth knowing: to close port 3000, Agent removed and recreated my container. That was harmless for a stateless demo, but a container that keeps data without a volume would lose it. Take a snapshot before big jobs, and keep the panel in view.

Here are the four plans at their Black Friday rates for a 24-month term.

For scale, here’s what my test used. With the Node app, Docker and Caddy all running, KVM 1 had 3.3 GB of its 4 GB RAM still available, and the app container itself used about 65 MB.

So 4 GB is plenty for one or two small apps. Move to 8 GB on KVM 2 once you add Coolify, a database, or n8n with its own Postgres, because those are the services that eat memory.

Finding a cheap VPS got harder in 2026, because memory got expensive. AI data centres are buying up DRAM, and hosts are passing the cost on. In April 2026, Hetzner raised cloud server prices by up to 37%, citing hardware costs, then raised prices again in June. On the same April date, OVHcloud’s entry VPS went from $4.90 to $7.60, a 55% jump. Nobody can promise where VPS prices go next, but the trend this year has been up.

This is how Hostinger’s Black Friday pricing works. You pay upfront for 12 or 24 months, and the 24-month term gives the lowest monthly rate. Your price is fixed for that whole term. After that, it renews at the standard rate.

On KVM 2, for example, you pay $210.96 today (the $8.79/mo rate) for 24 months, and it renews at $14.99/mo after that. Do the maths on the renewal before you commit, not just the headline rate.

A “Find My Air” setting uses Bluetooth strength you help you track down a lost tracker.

While a big part of the appeal of Fitbit Air is its screenless, disconnected nature, having the option to set up alerts for important apps or contacts is certainly a useful option.

“Air Relay” is free on Google Play, and Fitbit Air is available for $99.

#10384. Is Kling 4.0 the Right AI Video Generator for Your Project?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A short clip can help a project in different ways. It can show an idea you have not filmed, put an existing design in motion, or give an announcement something to look at. The same approach will not work if the clip needs to demonstrate a real feature or show exactly what happened. Before choosing a generator, decide what the viewer needs to learn from the shot.

This article uses Kling 4.0 as a worked example, but the decision process applies to any AI video generator. It covers how these tools turn prompts and images into video, how Kling 4.0 and a few alternatives differ, how to choose a starting mode, how to add exact text and branding outside the generated clip with a short ffmpeg script, and when real footage is the better choice. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

● Generated video is best for imagined shots. It suits concepts, backgrounds and early explorations, not instructions or faithful records.

● Your starting material decides the mode. A written idea, a reference image or a pair of opening and closing frames each call for a different input.

● Keep exact wording out of the generated clip. Titles, dates and logos belong on separate layers in an editor.

● Tools differ on control, cost and licensing. Compare inputs, length, resolution, credits and terms before you commit.

● Always review. Watch the whole clip at its final size and check it against the real product or design.

Most current systems extend diffusion models across time. A model learns to turn noise into a coherent set of frames guided by a prompt, and sometimes by an image or a clip. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequences are easy to observe.

● The prompt describes change over time. One visible change and one camera instruction usually work better than a long list of actions.

● Reference material steers but does not lock. A picture can suggest shape and colour without guaranteeing an exact match.

● Longer clips give more room for drift. Faces, logos and fine text are the first things to change between frames.

● Cost grows with length and resolution. Most hosted tools charge credits per second, so short previews are cheaper than full shots.

If you only have an idea, start with text. For a short announcement, you might ask for a band of light to move across a wall of coloured tiles. No one needs to recognise a particular wall, so the model can decide how it looks. Give it one visible change and a clear view of the subject. A prompt such as Soft morning light travels slowly across a wall of blue ceramic tiles. Fixed camera, no lettering is enough to see whether the idea works.

If you already have an illustration or a photo, reference mode gives you another starting point. Suppose you want a short concept clip for a desk accessory that only exists as a drawing. The picture can show the general shape and colour while the prompt describes the view you want. A video reference can help when it is the motion that is hard to explain, such as a slow camera slide. Say what each asset is meant to guide, and do not add a clip just because you have one.

Use keyframes when a particular view matters at a particular moment. Start with an opening image. If a short reveal also needs to end with the object facing forward, add an ending image to show that view. That is different from a reference image that mainly shows what the object looks like. Neither method guarantees an exact match, so check the result rather than assuming the uploaded picture has locked the design.

No single tool fits every project. The table summarizes what each option’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

Reading the table: hosted studios such as the one on JXP are the quickest way to test an idea and need no setup, but you pay per second and work within their supported inputs. Kling’s own site is the place to check announcements and API options directly, since its page says little about durations or resolution. Runway positions its models for motion quality and prompt following, while Google’s Gemini API video documentation suits teams building generation into their own products. The Diffusers video guide covers open-source pipelines for developers who want local control and are ready to manage GPUs and licences.

Trying an idea before making it. An illustrator might want to see how an imagined landscape feels when the light changes. A quick clip gives the team something to discuss: is the change visible, and does it happen at the right pace? It does not need to be the final animation to answer those questions.

Showing a product concept. A rotating view or a slow camera move can help people discuss how a design might be presented. It is useful early, when the shape and lighting are still being explored. Once the clip represents a product someone can buy, the standard changes. If a port moves or a button appears that the real product does not have, it is the wrong clip for that job.

Making a short visual for a post. A few seconds of moving colour can give an event announcement or a project update a distinctive opening. Put the name, date and other exact wording in your editor so they are readable and easy to change. The background only needs to support the message. If it competes with the text, use less movement or keep the background still.

Generated video is unreliable at lettering, so add titles in an editor or with a script. The command below overlays a title on a generated background and then checks the file properties. It assumes you already have a clip named background.mp4 and ffmpeg installed.

-vf "drawtext=text='Launch Night | 12 March':fontcolor=white:fontsize=64:x=(w-text_w)/2:y=(h-text_h)/2:box=1:boxcolor=black@0.35:boxborderw=24" \

-c:v libx264 -pix_fmt yuv420p -an announcement.mp4

-show_entries stream=width,height,r_frame_rate,duration \

-of default=noprint_wrappers=1 announcement.mp4

Because the text lives in the command, you can reuse the same background for three events by changing one string. The font may need to be set with a fontfile option on some systems. This is the practical version of keeping the moving background in one file and the editable details in another.

If people will follow the clip as instructions, use real footage or a screen recording for the steps. A convincing shot of an app is not proof that a button is in the right place. A generated close-up of a product is not a reliable way to show how its parts fit. You can still use an imagined opening, but make the switch to the real demonstration clear.

Think about revisions, too. If your team expects to adjust the same title, logo or date every week, put those elements on separate layers in an editor. And if the only movement you need is a slow zoom on an approved picture, apply that zoom directly. There is no reason to risk changing the picture when a simple edit will do.

This matters when someone else will maintain the video. A reusable announcement template is easier to hand over if the moving background is one file and the event details are editable. If the team likes the movement but needs three versions with different dates, they can reuse the same clip. If every change calls for a new generation, the time you saved on the first version may disappear on the second.

The Kling 4.0 video generator on JXP currently offers 720p and 1080p settings and clip lengths from 3 to 30 seconds. According to its page, reference mode accepts up to 10 images and 5 videos, keyframe mode takes a first frame, middle frames and an optional last frame, and credits are charged per second with a higher rate at 1080p. For a first attempt, choose the frame shape you plan to use and make the clip only as long as it needs to be. Check the displayed credit cost before generating, because a short preview can tell you whether the direction works before you spend on a longer shot.

Decide what a good result looks like before you generate it. For the tile background, you need to see the light move without distracting from the words you will add. For a product concept, check whether the shape stays recognisable as the viewpoint changes. Watch the whole clip at the size the audience will see it. If the shot misses the point, revise one thing at a time: the motion, the framing or the source image.

● Read the licence. JXP’s page lists a commercial use license on each plan and says outputs are for creative and lawful use, with the user responsible for reviewing them. Other tools have different terms.

● Use inputs you have the right to use. Do not upload other people’s copyrighted work or photos of people without permission.

● Do not misrepresent products or events. A generated clip should not suggest features or footage that do not exist.

● Consider provenance. C2PA is an open standard for Content Credentials that records where content came from and how it was edited, and some tools support it.

● Disclose where required. Follow platform and advertising rules on labelling AI-generated or altered content.

● Fine text, logos, hands and fast motion can drift or flicker between frames.

● Reference images and keyframes steer the result but do not guarantee an exact match.

● Vendor specifications, such as resolution and length, can differ between a model announcement and a particular platform.

● Credit costs and plan terms change, so check them on the day you generate.

● Long or exact sequences are often cheaper and safer to film than to generate.

Short imagined shots, concept clips and moving backgrounds, where some details can be left open and the clip is reviewed before use.

Use text when you only have an idea, reference mode when you have a picture or clip to guide the look, and keyframes when specific views matter at specific moments.

Not reliably. Use real footage or a screen recording for instructions and demonstrations.

No. Hosted studios, vendor sites, developer APIs and open-source pipelines all exist. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your project.

Kling 4.0 is a good candidate when the missing piece is an imagined shot and you can describe what it needs to do. Pick one such shot, try it, and put it next to the material you already have, and compare at least one alternative before you commit. If the project needs exact instructions or a faithful record, use the real thing. A useful first result is one that does its job in the edit, not simply one that looks impressive on its own.

The latest in 3D printing tech comes to Frankfurt.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Once a year, all the world’s 3D printing manufacturers gather for Formnext in Frankfurt, Germany, to showcase new technologies, software, and ideas. This year’s show was more compact, with three halls instead of four, but consumer 3D printers had a larger presence. Spread across two halls, we found Bambu Lab, Prusa Research, Snapmaker, Creality, Elegoo, Anycubic, and Flashforge, plus a few newbies like Axiloop.

Startup SnowX was a popular booth, presenting the first consumer-grade desktop metal 3D printer coming soon to Kickstarter.

I counted ten tool-changing 3D printers on the show floor, five of which I’ve already reviewed. Elegoo surprised us all by launching its Centauri X Ultra four-color tool changer at the show, while Axiloop’s MC 8 arrived on my front porch that morning.

Only Anycubic is standing pat with a single-nozzle multicolor machine. Instead, it presented its new partnership with Thingiverse.

Full-color UV printers were on display as well, with Flashforge showing off its CJ270, which was teased last year.

Editor’s Note: Snapmaker provided travel accommodations for this trip. Coverage remains editorially independent.

Bambu Lab showed off its latest printers, the H3C, a tool changer (starting at $2,499) with nine lightweight tools we reviewed earlier in the month, and its first laser, the R1, reviewed in September, which retails at $2,499.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

More interesting is the launch of “Bambu Circle,” a pilot program that will start in the Netherlands and slowly expand across Europe. The goal is a network of local hubs to assist entrepreneurs with their 3D printing side hustle. Bambu Circle would enlist repair specialists and community ambassadors to provide a helping hand and an in-person retail shop with space for meetups. ,

At the moment, there is no plan to launch a similar program in the United States; however, we have many retailers here who offer official warranty support, parts, and tons of filament. A map on the Bambu Lab lists 31 US resellers, though that number is a bit skewed since there are also 31 MicroCenters in the US.

Prusa Research previewed the Rugged, a CORE One L+ adapted for printing in the harshest environments. The printer has a sealed case that keeps out both humidity and dust while providing extra protection during transport for machines used in the field. The CORE One L+ tucked inside is equipped with a high-temperature, hardened steel nozzle for printing advanced engineering filaments. It also comes with Prusa’s Zero-Trace system, which is an air-gapped machine with no flash drive storage, no USB slot, no Wi-Fi, and no camera. They can only accept files from your own cryptographically paired Prusa Connect server. These extremely secure, extremely tough machines are meant for classified and proprietary work environments.

However, you don’t need to have a security clearance to jump on board Prusa Connect Local, a free-to-use version of Prusa Connect for anyone who wishes to keep their 3D printers off the internet. The server can run on Linux, Windows, Mac, or a Raspberry Pi, and can handle a print farm of two to 400. It’s currently set up for any Prusa Printer, but it is open source, so you are free to tinker around with it and add your own extensions.

Snapmaker showed off what you can do with its four-color U1 tool changer. Ratdoux, the developer behind Full Spectrum, was on hand to chat with us. He devised a system of color-blending filaments that take thin layers of cyan, yellow, and magenta mixed with either gray or white, which can create nearly any other color. He said it was inspired by a conversation on Reddit where users were mixing colors in Blender, and he came up with a simpler way to do it in the slicer. Snapmaker immediately hired him to lead a development team for its fork of OrcaSlicer.

Amazingly, Full Spectrum went from an experimental slicer hack to a must-have for every tool changer in the space of nine months. Snapmaker’s Full Spectrum is tailored for the U1 on open-source OrcaSlicer, while Bambu Lab installed a “Color Mixing” tool in Bambu Studio and Prusa Research developed their own “ColorMix” technology based on hex codes.

Full Spectrum and similar tools can be used on any multimaterial 3D printer. However, because it looks best with super thin layers, tool changers are more efficient and less wasteful.

Snapmaker has since introduced a Full Spectrum filament bundle optimized for four-color machines: a CMYW for lighter, bright colors and a CMYG (gray edition) for darker shades. Ratdoux said the secret is in the filament’s light Transmission Distance (TD), which measures how translucent the material is. Colors that allow more light to pass through them create a better illusion of blending. The parrot and fox pictured above were printed using CMYG and a .08mm layer height. There was no orange, purple, green or black filament used in those prints.

We also found Axiloop, an eight-color tool changer that’s already raised over $700,000 on Kickstarter. The fully enclosed CoreXY machine has a 300x300mm build plate, 4-second tool swaps, and claims to have “near-zero” wipe-free tool changes. Its price for the MC8 Pro is $1,499 for early backers, while the open frame MC8 is $1,099.

Our review unit arrived while I was at Formnext, so I’m excited to put it to the test soon.

Peopoly’s Giga 800 is a large-scale pellet feeder that’s a bit north of our normal prosumer range at $15,000, but I felt it was worth mentioning because it's from our friends at Siraya Tech, who make superior filaments and resin. I noticed quite a few YouTubers happily climb into the machine to show how big it truly is.

The machine has an extrusion output of three kilos an HOUR, which would be equivalent to three spools of normal filament, and can use a 3mm nozzle for chonky prints. We’ve reviewed a similar-sized open-frame “giga” machine before, so I know that being hampered by a normal .4mm nozzle is not something you want with a print volume this large. The enclosed machine is capable of running everything from PLA and TPU to PET-CF and PPA-GF.

Creality had quite a few machines out, but the most interesting were the Creality Falcon T1 UV Laser we reviewed this summer and the new Falcon A1C portable laser we’ll be reviewing soon. The T1 is able to do inner engraving in glass in just a few minutes, and the team was happy to show me the workflow after I supplied them with a photo. They took my selfie, ran it through Creality’s AI to create a 3D bust, then zapped it into a 2 x 3 inch chunk of glass. It was an impressive demo.

They also showed off several handheld scanners, which Creality feels will enable makers to create more practical prints without needing a ton of CAD skills. My favorite is the Creality Pika we reviewed in October.

I’m also particularly intrigued by the mBox kid printer, with a small 153x153mm build plate but lots of safety features to make it child-friendly. The printer is priced at $329, which isn’t bad for a full-feature printer. However, good luck finding one to buy: this printer is only available on the TikTok Shop in the US until December 10.

Another disappointment was the Creality M1 filament maker with its companion R1 shredder. The unit was on display, but not running, which makes me believe they haven’t quite figured out how to make the dream of home filament making a reality.

Elegoo surprised us all with the Centauri X Ultra, a four-color tool changer variation of the wildly successful Centauri Carbon. The Centauri X Ultra is scheduled to release early in 2027. It has a 256x256mm bed, making it the same size as its siblings. The machine is completely enclosed and cleverly contains the side-mounted spools in a Tupperware-like sealed box. The machine has filament drying and a nozzle that can hit 350C, making it a good candidate for high-temperature filaments. It also claims to have 5-second color swaps without waste, but we’ll test that as soon as we get our hands on one.

FlashForge revealed its CJ270 full-color 3D printer, priced at $ 3,999 and now shipping. It is listed as a MultiJet Printer, which uses UV ink to build up models, with a build size of 180 x 120 x 100 mm. Unlike other UV printers, the CJ270 has seven printheads (one for each ink channel), so if one gets clogged, you don’t need to replace the entire printhead. It uses CMYK ink, plus white, clear, and a water-washable support material. The sample models shown were impressive, with lots of detail and strong color.

Anycubic didn’t have any new machines to show off, but they did have a huge announcement to make: they are collaborating with Thingiverse to bring millions of models into Anycubic’s file-sharing library, MakerOnline. Select designs will be converted into multicolor models for one-click printing from Anycubic’s mobile app.

The company is also looking into adding an AI tool dubbed AnyLab to its website to help users create their own designs.
