#10376. OnePlus starts shutting down its website to ‘focus on customer support’

After shutting down its US operations earlier this year, OnePlus has quietly closed its website in some regions as it shifts to “focus on customer support.”

OnePlus announced in July that it would end new product launches in the US and Europe. Just weeks later, the OnePlus 15 and other products the brand was selling in the US were sold out for good and, more recently, OnePlus rolled out its Android 17 update to transition devices to Oppo’s ColorOS instead of OxygenOS.

Now, OnePlus has fully pulled the plug on its US website, as well as some other regions.

oneplus.com/us has purged all product listings, including for accessories, with a new message saying that the brand is “streamlining” the site:

Following our July 16, 2026 announcement, OnePlus is streamlining this website to focus on customer support for existing OnePlus devices. For warranty, repair, and other support, please visit our support site Click.

The same applies to the OnePlus website in the Canada, and other regions, though the UK and EU countries such as France still have an operational site that mostly push Oppo products over remaining OnePlus devices, a practice that started a few months ago prior to the shutdown. The OnePlus 15, for example, is no longer available to purchase in the UK at all. Presumably, though, the OnePlus website will shut down in these regions eventually as well.

The OnePlus website in India is unaffected and still selling devices.

The warranty website OnePlus links to appears to be more than a bit out of date, with a focus on 2023’s OnePlus Open. The most recent device mentioned is the OnePlus 13, though the page has been updated based on what we’re able to see on the Wayback Machine.

#10375. How Consumer AI Companions Handle Context Windows, Persistent Memory, and Voice Latency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI companions are the most demanding consumer use of large language models right now. A coding assistant can forget your last message and still be useful. A companion that forgets your name, your running story, or the plan you made last Tuesday has failed at its one job. On top of that, users now expect voice calls that feel like a phone conversation, not a walkie-talkie exchange with a five-second pause.

Three engineering problems decide whether a companion app works: how much of the conversation fits in the model's context window, what the app remembers once the window fills up, and how fast the voice pipeline answers. Here is how consumer companions handle each one, with real tier and pricing examples, and what developers can take from their designs for their own products.

Most LLM products are judged on single answers. Companions are judged on continuity. That creates pressure in three places at once:

Every consumer companion is a different set of trade-offs across those three constraints, and the trade-offs are increasingly how the apps are priced.

The context window is the total number of tokens the model can see when it writes the next reply. In a companion app, that budget is spent before the user says anything:

When the chat log outgrows what is left, the oldest messages fall out. The user experiences this as the companion "forgetting" mid-conversation. This is why context size is now a headline spec in the category, and a paid feature. SpicyChat AI, for example, gives free accounts a 4K context window, then scales memory to 8K on its $19.95/mo tier and 32K on its $29.95/mo tier. Janitor AI runs about 9K of context on its own JanitorLLM. Independent uncensored AI chat benchmarks record these context sizes alongside refusal behavior and pricing, because context length predicts how long a roleplay stays coherent better than any marketing claim.

Some apps sell memory in messages rather than tokens. Fantasy GF's pricing page lists a 60-message base "smart memory" and 5x that, 300 messages, on its Ultra plan. Others sell it as a memory multiplier on higher subscription tiers. The pattern is consistent across the category: memory depth is the upgrade lever, not the model itself.

No consumer app keeps an entire relationship in the prompt. Persistent memory is a separate system that writes to storage and reads back into the context window on each turn. In practice it has three layers:

The differences users notice are usually retention policies, not algorithms. Crushon AI's own subscription docs say free-plan chat history is deleted after 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans keep it for the membership period. Paid tiers across the category commonly promise that memories never expire. There is also a security angle worth stating plainly: companion chat logs are among the most sensitive data a consumer product can hold. A 2024 breach at Muah.ai, first reported by 404 Media, exposed roughly 1.9 million users' email addresses together with their private prompts. Any app that builds persistent memory is also building a liability, and developers copying the pattern should treat the memory store as the most protected part of the system.

A voice call in a companion app is three models chained together: speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech. Run them strictly in sequence and a single reply can take several seconds, which reads as dead air. The apps that feel fast attack each stage the same way:

With every stage streaming, time-to-first-audio can land close to a second on a good connection, which is roughly the threshold where a conversation stops feeling laggy. Voice is also expensive to run - three model calls instead of one, plus audio bandwidth - which is why it is metered harder than text. Fantasy GF charges 8 tokens per minute for voice calls (4 on Ultra), which turns the 100 monthly tokens on its entry plan into about 12 minutes of call time. SpicyChat, for its part, only adds text-to-speech at its top tier. Latency engineering and unit economics are the same problem here: faster pipelines cost less per call, and cheaper calls are what make voice viable below the top plan.

Tier details below come from each app's own pricing or documentation pages, checked November 16, 2026, and cross-checked against il.ly's AI companion testing guide, which hands-tests 15 companion apps across memory, media, and voice.

Two takeaways from the table. First, nobody sells a bigger base model - they sell a bigger window into the same one. Second, the free tiers are deliberately memory-poor: limited context and short retention are the upgrade prompt.

Even if you never build a companion, these apps are a useful reference architecture for any stateful LLM product:

Consumer AI companions have turned three hard infrastructure problems - bounded context, durable memory, and real-time voice - into their pricing pages. Context size, memory depth, and voice access are the tiers. If you are building anything stateful on an LLM, this category is worth watching closely: it is solving continuity and latency problems at consumer scale, in public, one subscription tier at a time.

It is the total amount of text, measured in tokens, that the model can consider when writing its next reply. The character definition, stored memories, recent chat, and the new message all share that budget. When the conversation exceeds it, the oldest content drops out and the companion appears to forget.

Yes, through a memory system separate from the context window. Apps summarize older chats, extract stable facts about you into a profile, and retrieve relevant past moments into the prompt. Retention varies: some free tiers delete chat history after as little as 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans typically keep memory for the membership period.

A voice turn runs three models - speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech - instead of one, plus audio transfer. Providers pass that cost on through per-minute token charges or by locking voice behind higher tiers.

For casual chat, 4K to 9K tokens works. For long-running roleplay or a companion expected to hold plot threads for weeks, 32K and up, or a well-built memory layer on top of a smaller window, makes the visible difference.

U.S. senators and representatives want to know how Nvidia is dealing with reports of AI chip smuggling into China.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Even though Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as its CEO earlier this week under pressure from President Donald Trump, members of the U.S. House of Representatives and the U.S. Senate say that he must still appear before them and testify as they dive into their respective investigations. The Select Committee on China posted on X that the Nvidia CEO and the company’s general counsel, Tim Teter, are set to face the committee on December 1 and 2, respectively, while Bloomberg reports that Jensen will publicly testify before the powerful U.S. Senate Committee on Banking, Housing, and Urban Affairs on December 3.

Statement on Jensen Huang's Reinstatement at NVIDIA Chairmen John Moolenaar (R-MI) of the Select Committee on China, Rick Crawford (R-AR) of the Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence, and Brian Mast (R-FL) of the Foreign Affairs Committee jointly released the following statement concerning Jensen Huang's reinstatement by NVIDIA’s board: “The NVIDIA board’s brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed. Jensen Huang and Tim Teter have confirmed they will appear for their transcribed interviews on December 1 and December 2 as previously planned. We remain committed to getting answers on the mass smuggling of NVIDIA products, their diversion for Chinese military purposes, and their key role in advancing our foremost adversary’s AI capabilities."November 23, 2026

“The NVIDIA board’s brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed,” the Select Committee on China said. “Jensen Huang and Tim Teter have confirmed they will appear for their transcribed interviews on December 1 and December 2 as previously planned. We remain committed to getting answers on the mass smuggling of NVIDIA products, their diversion for Chinese military purposes, and their key role in advancing our foremost adversary’s AI capabilities.”

The chairpersons of the House Committees on China, Foreign Affairs, and Intelligence first called upon the Nvidia CEO on November 7 for a closed-door meeting to discuss the allegations that he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, which lawmakers say “raise significant national security concerns.” Just a day later, Jensen admitted to having an affair, although he did not mention the name of the third party. On the same day that the Nvidia board reinstated Mr. Huang, Senate Banking Committee Chairperson Tim Scott said that Jensen will appear before the committee and publicly testify during a hearing about U.S. export controls on advanced AI hardware.

One of the biggest issues that Jensen Huang is facing is the numerous reports of chip diversion involving Nvidia’s products. The U.S. first put export controls on Nvidia H100 chips in 2022 as it feared that China was using it for military purposes. However, there have been rumors that you could easily get these chips in China as long as you know who to ask as early as 2023, even though the Nvidia CEO answered, “There’s no evidence of any chip diversion,” when asked about it one time. Furthermore, Alice Huang, one of the people that he had an affair with, was allegedly instrumental in securing over $7.5 billion worth of illicit Nvidia GPU imports to China. Jensen Huang himself is known to be against export controls and has called them a failure.

The Select Committee on China’s meeting with the Nvidia CEO will be conducted privately. Although there is going to be a transcription of the interview, it’s unclear if and when the data will be released publicly. On the other hand, the Senate Banking Committee hearing is open to the public and will be broadcast live and online, which will also be the first time that Jensen Huang will face a formal congressional hearing.

#10372. I love YouTube on TV, but we desperately need better watchlist controls

Managing a YouTube watchlist shouldn’t be as frustrating as it is, especially on the most-used app in my life. Google’s premier streaming app has too many issues with resurfacing content; in my opinion, this is now a major problem that needs to be fixed.

Aside from my YouTube watchlist gripes, it’s no secret that moving from a web browser to the TV is ultimately still a frustrating experience.

Lots of things I love about watching videos on my laptop or PC just don’t translate well to when I’m sat on my sofa holding a remote trying to find something to watch after wrapping up for the day. One thing I’m getting annoyed with is how YouTube scales on TV and Android TV boxes because you’re given less choice and arguably poorer recommendations for content due to the constraints in place.

Understandably, you can’t compress the layout too much on a TV as you’re typically sat a few feet away and all aspects of the interface need to be legible. I would still like to tweak it, though, if that were possible.

Maybe we could get proper sections for content, like how Netflix and other streaming platforms offer categorized areas where you can search for “comedy” or “horror”. I’m almost certain that, with Google’s insane investment and massive AI push, where every video is already analyzed hundreds of times, that wouldn’t be too difficult to do.

The problem now is that the sidebar menu is just too broad. Gaming covers basically everything. Then each line of videos accounts for one title, but requires search and previous watch data to populate. I find myself often fighting with recommendations on my TV, whereas on desktop it’s not as much of a problem.

That leads me to my biggest problem with YouTube, and it’s the, frankly, embarrassing watchlist or “Watch later” controls.

I can’t fault that it works, but right now the watchlist function is like something out of the early 2000s due to the lack of flexibility that I touched on above. As my YouTube consumption has increased 20x over the past four or five years, managing what I want to come back to or might have missed the first time around is starting to become a bit of a chore.

I’ve tried using playlists to save things, but things get messy very quickly because of how cumbersome it is to a) create a playlist on TV and b) how playlists are organized by default.

Google TV isn’t perfect, but it’s where I keep my biggest TV and movie watchlist outside of YouTube. Yes, YouTube is mostly for user-generated content, but I would take something as basic as Google TV’s interface baked into YouTube somehow. Add in categorization and auto-clear.

The Watch Later section has become an unwieldy place that I never really want to go unless I desperately have to. I’m constantly losing videos that I hoped to come back to or have an affinity for.

Videos don’t clear automatically, nor do you know when they were added. Yes, I’ll give credit that you can sort by a few options, but that’s the bare minimum. The process of moving things around is slow and takes multiple taps. Watch Later is also hidden compared to other services. It’s under your account, then beneath “Liked videos,” which is below any you may have uploaded yourself.

On a TV, the process is so annoying I just avoid it altogether. You go to the sidebar menu > Library > Watch later. It should be front-and-center on a TV. It’s almost unforgivable in my eyes because even your watch history is given greater prominence. I simply cannot understand that decision.

I’m not always a huge fan of AI being shoehorned into apps and services, but this is one of those areas that has been left to rot for a while and could get a big uplift from adding in a little bit of Gemini. Even if it were just an enhanced search function or more tools for organizing.

With YouTube TV effectively taking over regular broadcast television and being the world’s biggest streaming platform, I’m shocked by how so many areas of the platform remain in a consistently neglected state.

A little bit of spit and polish, and maybe I’ll start using this section again. For now, though, I’ll stick to texting myself links of stuff I want to watch. The ball is in your court, Google.

#10365. Serving a Local LLM to Several Users: Ollama, LM Studio, llama.cpp and vLLM Benchmarked

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When one person chats with a local model, it barely matters which server you run. On one RTX 3090 with the same model file, Ollama, LM Studio and llama.cpp wrote text within about 10 percent of each other, because all three run llama.cpp underneath. That changes when your app sends several requests at once. With four requests, the best setup produced more than three times as much text as the worst, and the cause was a default setting, not the engine. With 32 requests, vLLM produced 2.5 times as much text as the best llama.cpp setup I tested.

This article puts those numbers side by side and shows which setting to change for your app.

I ran every test on one machine. The full write-ups, with every command and the raw results, are on homebrewserver.club.

Ollama, LM Studio and llama.cpp loaded the exact same GGUF files, checked by SHA-256. vLLM cannot run GGUF files well, so it loaded a 4-bit AWQ file of the same model (9.1 GB). Answer quality was not compared.

The tests ran between 8 and 16 November 2026, and speeds on this machine varied by a few percent from one day to the next. Treat a gap under about 5% as a tie. All of this is one consumer GPU and one model family, so read the numbers as a guide to how the servers behave, not as a ranking for your hardware.

This is the short prompt with a 512-token answer.

Ollama and LM Studio both start llama.cpp's llama-server program for each model they load. Ollama has done this since version 0.30, and a comment in its source code says so. On Linux, LM Studio's CUDA runtime is a folder with llama.cpp libraries and a llama-server binary.

Ollama logs the command it starts when you set OLLAMA_DEBUG=1. When llama.cpp was started by hand with that exact command, in a run next to Ollama, its speed matched Ollama's within 1%. So Ollama adds no speed cost of its own. LM Studio was 5 to 10% slower in this test. The test did not separate whether that came from its llama.cpp build or its other start options.

For one user, the gap between Ollama and LM Studio was about 0.4 s on a 512-token answer from the 9B model. You would notice it in long answers, and hardly in a chat window.

A web app rarely sends one request at a time. A chat page, a background summary job and an agent can all call the same model at the same moment. The next test sent four requests at once, each asking for 256 tokens, and measured the total output per second.

A slot is one request that the server works on at the same time as others. llama-server sets the number of slots with -np (short for --parallel), and Ollama and LM Studio pass their own setting through to it.

With one slot, the server answers the four requests one after another, so the total stays at the speed of a single request. That is Ollama's default. With four slots, the server puts the four requests through the model together, and the total rose to more than twice that of one slot. That is the default in LM Studio and llama.cpp. vLLM was the fastest at four requests, but it was tested only with the 9B model. Its lead grows with more requests, as the section after next shows.

For your app, this means that the fourth user of a default Ollama server waits for the first three answers to finish before their own answer starts.

The obvious fix is OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=4. It worked for the 9B model, though in one of three rounds Ollama still answered at the speed of a single request. With the 27B model, it made Ollama slower than its default.

The cause is how each server sizes the context, the memory that holds each request's prompt and answer. Ollama picks the context from the GPU's memory: 32,768 tokens on a 24 GB card, according to its docs. With four slots, it gives each slot its own 32,768 tokens, so it started llama-server with a 131,072-token context. The Ollama FAQ says the memory needed grows with the number of parallel requests multiplied by the context length.

That context did not fit next to the 27B model in 24 GB. ollama ps showed 16% of the model on the CPU, and the total speed fell to 33.1 tokens per second.

LM Studio and llama.cpp handle it differently. Their four slots share one pool of context:

The price of LM Studio's default is a short context. Four requests sharing 8,192 tokens leave little room for long documents or a long chat history. If your app sends long prompts, load the model with a larger context.

In Ollama, setting a smaller context with OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH next to OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL should avoid the spill to the CPU. The test did not try this, so measure it on your own card.

The last test went further, from 1 to 32 requests at once, with the 9B model. It compared vLLM, a server built to serve one model to many users, with llama.cpp at its defaults and with more slots. Ollama and LM Studio were tested only up to four requests, so they are not in this table.

With its defaults, llama-server chose four slots by itself, so it worked on four requests and queued the rest. Its total stayed near 330 tokens per second from 4 requests to 32. Setting -np 16 or -np 32 roughly doubled the total at 16 requests.

vLLM's lead came from each request running faster inside a large batch. At 32 requests, each vLLM request generated about 61 tokens per second, against about 27 on llama.cpp with 32 slots. vLLM's README names the two techniques behind this: PagedAttention, which stores each request's working memory in small blocks, and continuous batching, which adds new requests to the batch the GPU is already working on.

Total output tells you how much work the server does. The wait for the first word tells you how the app feels to the user behind the last request:

For a web app, that is 22.5 seconds of spinner before any text appears. Long prompts make every wait longer, on both servers. With eight requests of about 4,120 tokens each, the last request waited 7.9 s on vLLM and 8.8 s on llama.cpp with 32 slots.

If you set the slots yourself, the llama-server README lists three options that work together:

If you run an older llama.cpp, update first. Four requests at once ran 14 to 22% faster on v1.0.0 than on the build from five days earlier, while one request stayed the same. The most likely reason is a new CUDA kernel in v1.0 for steps of 2 to 24 tokens on RTX 30-series and newer cards.

vLLM's throughput comes with two costs that matter on a shared machine.

It reserves most of the GPU. By default, vLLM takes 92% of the card's memory before it loads anything, and fills what the model does not need with cache. With a desktop and a browser holding about 2 GiB, vLLM refused to start:

Setting --gpu-memory-utilization 0.6 alone was not enough with this model, because the default limit of 256 sequences needed more cache than fit. Adding --max-num-seqs 32 fixed it. With both settings, vLLM held 12.7 GiB and kept the same speeds. llama.cpp works the other way: it sizes its cache from -c and -np and never asked for more than 15.1 GiB in this test.

It starts slowly. llama-server answered requests 2 to 5 s after its container started. vLLM compiles the model before it serves anything. That took 72 s with its compile cache already on disk, and 173 s on the first start without it. Changing a vLLM argument triggered a fresh compile.

It also needs a different model file. vLLM loads Hugging Face safetensors, so a 4-bit model is a separate AWQ or GPTQ upload. The AWQ file here was 9.1 GB against 5.7 GB for the GGUF, because its recipe leaves some layers unquantized. Check the file size, not only "4-bit", when you size a model for your card.

All four servers have an OpenAI-compatible API under /v1, so the same client code works with each one. You change the base URL and the model name. These are the ports used in the tests:

In Node.js with the openai package, that looks like the sketch below. The measurements came from a Python test client, and this snippet was not part of them:

Qwen3.5 thinks before it answers unless you turn it off. The tests sent "chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": false} in the request body to vLLM and llama.cpp.

None of the servers asked for a password in these tests. llama-server also warned at start-up that its CORS rule allowed all origins. Bind the port to 127.0.0.1, as in the table, and set --api-key on llama.cpp or vLLM if anything else on your network can reach it.

Before you choose, count how many requests your app really sends at the same moment. Log the timestamps for a day, or count the parallel calls your agent makes per user action. If that number is one, any of these servers will do. If it is four or more, the slot setting will change your users' wait more than the choice of engine.

Not for one request with the same settings. Ollama runs llama.cpp's own server, and the two were within 1% when started with the same command. llama.cpp was faster with four requests at once, because it starts four slots by default and Ollama starts one.

Ollama gives each parallel request its own full context. With four slots on a 24 GB card, that was 131,072 tokens, which pushed 16% of a 27B model onto the CPU. A smaller context may avoid that, but this test did not measure it.

When many requests arrive together. At 32 requests, vLLM produced 2.5 times the output of llama.cpp with 32 slots and kept every first-word wait under a second. For one request at a time, llama.cpp was 5% faster and started in seconds instead of more than a minute.

Usually not. All four serve OpenAI-compatible endpoints under /v1, so you change the base URL and the model name.

Prosecutors have found recordings of meetings and conversations following the investigation into an OpenAI employee arrested for bugging the company's executive conference room.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Last month, a former OpenAI employee with ties to Saudi Arabia was arrested for bugging conference rooms in the company. Today, Bloomberg reports that prosecutors in the case uncovered hundreds of recordings, dozens of which were meetings and conversations dating as far back as September. Prosecutors believe some recordings may contain sensitive information that can be regarded as trade secrets, the dispersion of which could be a threat to US national security.

In October, we covered how Abdulrahman Alfozan, a former OpenAI employee, was arrested for installing a covert listening device behind the company's executive conference room television using a Seeed Xiao ESP32-S3. The device was discovered days later, with investigations linking it to Alfozan and subsequently leading to his arrest and removal from the company.

According to this latest report, prosecutors urged a federal judge to deny Alfozan’s request to be released on bail while the investigation is ongoing, fearing that Alfozan might flee before trial “given his relatively weak ties to the US and the considerable […] connections he has in his native Saudi Arabia.”

Prosecutors have stated, “Thus far, the government has located hundreds of audio files on the defendant’s personal computer, including dozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, many of which involve discussions of what may be trade secrets,” as reported by Bloomberg. The court filing also says that Alfozan kept audio files and AI-generated transcripts on his computer, with some recordings uploaded to Dropbox, “meticulously categorized by date and renamed according to the content of the recording.” Prosecutors also found a collections of images of a "strategic partnership" document marked as confidential on Alfozan's confiscated phone.

The filing also states, “At the same time as he was secretly recording, cataloging, and transcribing dozens of meetings concerning (among other things) OpenAI’s partnerships and capabilities, the defendant was in frequent communication with various people affiliated with the Saudi government.” However, Alfozan denies stealing trade secrets from OpenAI for Saudi Arabia, with his lawyer arguing that Alfozan “has no special or covert ties to the Saudi government,” in a separate court filing on Friday.

According to the October 27 indictment, he is charged with interception of oral communications, not theft of trade secrets or economic espionage, as with sharing trade secrets with another government. Alfozan requested to set his bond at $1 million, but this could be denied at the hearing set for Tuesday; additional charges could also follow if the investigation finds out what Alfozan is doing, or plans to do, with the recordings and to whom he would send them.

#10361. F-Secure Identifies 26 Ways AI Can Harm Consumers, and Where to Stop Them

New AI Harms Taxonomy traces how harm moves through AI systems, from misleading chatbot answers to compromised autonomous agents, and identifies where defenses can intervene.

LOS ANGELES (November 10, 2026) — AI now shapes how we search, communicate, decide and run our digital lives. But when it goes wrong, how does it actually hurt people? F-Secure’s new AI Harms Taxonomy maps 26 distinct harms to consumers, tracing the path each one takes to reach a person. The result is a clear view of where protections can step in, and who is in the best position to help.

Developed by Dr. Megan Squire, Principal Threat Intelligence Researcher at F-Secure, the taxonomy tackles a blind spot in how AI risk is discussed. Most frameworks list what could potentially go wrong, but stop short of connecting those harms to the systems and companies that could prevent them. F-Secure’s research goes further by mapping every layer between an AI-enabled threat and its impact on a consumer, then pinpointing where defenses need to go.

“As AI systems become more capable, the consequences of their mistakes can extend well beyond a bad answer,” said Dr. Squire. “The people building them have a responsibility to understand how they can fail and who gets hurt when they do, and to put safeguards in place before that harm happens.”

Related:Critical PHP Flaws Allow Code Execution, but Don't Panic 'PHP2Shell'-Style

The taxonomy reflects how the risks themselves are changing. It used to be a criminal going after a person directly, but now AI sits in the middle, which adds new players and new ways for things to go wrong. An assistant acting on someone’s behalf can be manipulated into doing damage, and a chatbot can mislead with complete confidence. Underneath, the trouble often starts in how an AI handles instructions and memory, or in the other services it’s connected to.

The research currently maps 26 harms across five relationship types and 19 intermediary layers, with 24 defenses designed to address risks at different points. Examples range from AI-generated voice and video impersonation to agents making harmful financial decisions, exposing personal information or acting on outdated context. It also examines attacks in which malicious instructions are hidden in content an AI system processes, potentially influencing what an agent does or what a chatbot tells a user.

One of the framework’s central findings is that responsibility for preventing AI harm is spread across several parties. Some risks can be handled by security software on a person’s own device. Others have to be dealt with further upstream, by the companies that build AI products and the systems behind them, including how an AI remembers things and which other services it connects to. Sorting out which is which makes it clear where a defense belongs and who has to build it. It also undercuts the idea that people can stay safe on their own just by spotting suspicious content or double-checking what an AI tells them.

Related:Cursor Traces Malicious Claude Code Extensions to Third-Party Breach

This question of who’s responsible gets more urgent as AI moves from producing information to acting on it. A chatbot that gets an answer wrong can mislead someone, but at least that person is part of the exchange. An agent with access to a person’s accounts and the services linked to them can cause real consequences without that kind of back-and-forth. Protections need to be built around what these systems actually do, which starts with understanding how the two differ.

This research is part of a wider effort at F-Secure to build trust into the AI era. F-Secure Trust, announced alongside a strategic alliance with Quantum AI Lab QuTwo, is the company’s work on protecting people in a world where AI agents increasingly act for them. TrustGuard is designed to help protect people during AI interactions, while TrustPath aims to secure their digital journeys from discovery to delivery. The AI Harms Taxonomy gives that work a research foundation by spelling out the risks the protections need to address, and it makes clear that meaningful safeguards also depend on the companies building and running AI systems.

Related:Grok Bots Attempt to Access Corporate Data Without User Approval

Today’s digital threats go far beyond just scams. F-Secure’s Illuminate Research team studies people and technology as they actually interact, exploring the messy realities of trust, deception, and online resilience in the AI era.

The AI Harms Taxonomy is available on the Illuminate Research Hub.

F-Secure is a human-first, AI-powered consumer cyber security experience company with 38 years of expertise in tackling digital threats. We help digital service providers turn trust into a high-value growth engine — protecting their customers while enabling them to live their best digital lives in a world of relentless, AI-driven scams. With billions of digital interactions secured each year, tens of millions of consumers protected globally, and over $10bn in partner value created, we deliver proven impact at scale.

When shopping for a graphics card, consider the following.

🔎 What resolution, settings do you want to play at? A low-end budget graphics card can play games at 1080p in medium settings, but if you want to play at ultra settings, you'll need to get at least a mid-range card. As you move up the stack of cards, you can play at 2K resolution and higher settings or, with the priciest cards, 4K.

🔎 How many FPS do you consider smooth? Most people consider 30 fps the bare minimum for playability and 60 fps decent. However, if you want less lag for eSports gaming, you'll want to be able to go to over 100 fps at reasonable settings. See our GPU benchmark hierarchy to find out how each GPU fares.

🔎 Do you have enough power? Make sure that you have enough capacity from your power supply to support the card. If you're not sure, use a tool such as Newegg's power supply calculator to see how much you need. If your power supply can't handle the card, either get a new PSU or a different card.

🔎 Will it fit in your case? If you've got a small PC case, make sure you check the length of the card against the case's clearance numbers.

#10354. GrapheneOS shows off how it plans RCS support without Google Messages

As virtually all Android phones and now iPhone support RCS messaging, the standard is key to communication in 2026. But GrapheneOS users, by default, are still stuck with SMS/MMS as RCS is typically locked behind Google Messages on Android.

It’s a problem that Graphene’s developers have been trying to work out for a while now, and it seems progress is being made. A proof-of-concept messaging app with RCS was shared on Twitter/X, showing Graphene’s RCS implementation working with both Google Messages and iOS with end-to-end encryption and full features including typing indicators and reactions.

While this app skips the need to use Google Messages directly, the app does still use Google’s services as most carriers rely on Google for RCS. GrapheneOS still needs a sandboxed version of Google Play, for now, for this to work, but the developers say that a standalone version for carriers that support Google-less RCS will be coming.

We made a proof of concept RCS messaging app. It already has end-to-end encryption with MLS compatible with Google Messages and iOS, typing indicators, delivery/read receipts, reactions and media/file sharing. #10353. 5 Things Most Developers Get Wrong When Turning Images into HTML

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Turning a design image into a working webpage can look simple.

You have a picture (a mockup, a screenshot, a Figma export), and your job is to turn it into working HTML in a browser.

But here is the catch. A design image is static, while a webpage is dynamic and responsive across different screen sizes and devices. A webpage needs to load quickly, adapt perfectly to different screen sizes, and allow touch interactions on mobile devices.

Most mistakes in this process happen when the conversion is treated as copying a picture instead of rebuilding it.

This section walks you through the mistakes developers often make, and more importantly, how to fix each of them.

Whether you are slicing a client mockup or converting a screenshot into code, avoiding these mistakes can save you from hours of rework. Let’s get into the details.

A screenshot reflects how a page appears at a particular display. But it doesn’t show how the layout will behave when the content changes or the screen gets smaller.

Developers sometimes try to copy the exact positions and dimensions of every element. To match the screenshot, they may use fixed widths, large margins, and absolute positioning.

This can work at one screen size, but the layout may break on smaller screens or when a heading wraps onto an extra line.

The better approach is identifying the layout rules behind the design. CSS Grid can arrange the cards in columns. Flexbox can help align elements within each card. And media queries can adjust the layout for smaller screens.

Pixel-level comparison can be useful in some cases, but it should not come at the cost of a flexible layout.

When developers recreate a design, it’s easier for them to focus on shapes and spacing rather than the purpose of each element. So, they may use generic elements for headings, buttons, navigation areas, and content sections instead of choosing elements that reflect each element's purpose.

This may produce the right appearance, but it often makes the code harder to maintain and understand. And when developers fail to use the right HTML elements, it can also create accessibility problems later.

So don’t put everything inside several unnamed containers. Instead, use a clear structure with appropriate headings, meaningful alt text for informative images, and real buttons for actions.

Semantic elements (e.g., header, nav, main, section, and footer) can also help organize a large page when they match the content’s purpose.

A design image may have headings, descriptions, prices, labels, or button text.

Reusing the entire image to reproduce the original design feels quick. But it can create problems when that text has to work as part of a responsive web page.

For example, there’s a promotional banner that contains a heading, a short description, and a CTA button. If you use the entire banner as one image, you can’t easily change the heading, translate the text, or make the button work as a real element.

So, don’t treat the image like that. Instead, use the original image where appropriate and recreate the text and button in HTML. CSS can control their position, size, color, and spacing.

Make sure you distinguish between visual elements that should remain images and text that should be recreated as HTML.

Not every part of turning images into HTML needs to be done manually.

Developers sometimes spend too much time on recreating basic elements when there are free online conversion tools available. These tools can help them get an initial version much faster.

An online conversion tool may be helpful when you need to extract text or generate a rough starting layout from a design image.

For example, you can upload a design image into an online image to html converter to get a starting point (rather than starting from a blank file). Review the tool’s output. Identify the parts you can reuse (as they are) and tweak the parts that need adjustments.

Keep in mind that the first result generated by the tool isn’t the final code. You may need to reorganize the HTML, refine the CSS, and adjust the layout to make it responsive.

Use automation where it saves time and your own judgement where it matters.

A screenshot shows just one view of a page. It doesn’t show how the layout may change on a phone or how people using a keyboard/screen reader can interact with it.

So, developers who focus only on the desktop design may end up with:

content that becomes too small on mobile devices

There’s also a chance of accessibility problems when images lack suitable alt text/interactive elements.

Let’s say a page has three cards in a row. On the desktop, this arrangement may work well. But on a phone, you may need to stack the cards vertically so their content remains readable.

That’s why accessibility should be considered during the same process. Use meaningful headings and provide appropriate alt text for images. Also, maintain sufficient text contrast and make sure interactive elements can be operated with a keyboard.

Lastly, don’t forget to test the page on both desktop and mobile screen sizes before finalizing it.

HTML and CSS handle the structure and appearance of a webpage, but JavaScript makes it possible to add interactive features.

For example, a design image may contain a navigation menu with a button. When recreating this design, developers should make sure the button performs an actual action rather than simply looking like a clickable element.

You can use JavaScript's event listeners to handle these interactions.

Now, add JavaScript to make the menu functional:

In this example, clicking the button opens or closes the navigation menu. The aria-expanded attribute also communicates the menu's current state to assistive technologies.

This is a simple example of why converting a design image into HTML involves more than matching its appearance. Developers also need to recreate the behavior of interactive elements using appropriate HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.

The process of turning an image into HTML feels easy when you treat it the right way.

Start by identifying the main sections, text, images, and interactive elements. Then create meaningful HTML and build the layout with CSS. Make the necessary adjustments to ensure it works across different screen sizes.

Use a conversion tool when it can really save time. But don’t forget to review the generated output of the tool thoroughly before using it.

A screenshot is a visual reference, not a complete set of development instructions. And the best result for you isn’t simply a page that looks like the image.

It is a page that also works well, adapts well, and stays easy to maintain.

Such is the case for anyone looking to purchase a 27-inch gaming monitor, which you can now find for well under $300. I’ve found a total of four 27-inch QHD monitors that are worth your attention (including the two mentioned above), so without further ado, let's see what’s cooking.

The Acer Predator X27U W1 is a 27-inch gaming monitor with a QHD resolution, 240 Hz refresh rate, and a QD-OLED panel. It supports AMD FreeSync Premium and includes two HDMI 2.1 and two DisplayPort 1.4 ports.

The Acer Predator X27U W1 features a 26.5-inch QD-OLED Penta Tandem panel. Acer claims that the Penta Tandem panel boosts luminous efficiency, doubles panel life, and allows for peak brightness of up to 1,000 nits (although typical brightness is closer to 200 nits).

You’ll find a 240 Hz refresh rate with support for AMD FreeSync Premium, 0.03 ms GtG response time, 99 percent coverage of the sRGB color space, an anti-glare coating over the display panel, and dual 3-watt speakers. On the connectivity front, you get two HDMI 2.1 ports, two DisplayPort 1.4 ports, and a single 3.5 mm headphone jack. The Predator X27U W1 is also adjustable for height, pivot, tilt, and swivel. The monitor rings up with a 14 percent discount at Amazon, bringing its final price to $269.99.

The Innocn 2780S is a 27-inch gaming monitor with a QHD resolution, 240 Hz refresh rate, and a QD-OLED panel. It covers 99% of sRGB and DCI-P3 and offers peak brightness of 400 nits.

The Innocn 2780S follows a similar formula with a matte screen finish, 0.03 ms GtG response time, and 99 percent sRGB coverage (and 99 percent for DCI-P3). However, the monitor doesn’t list any official AMD FreeSync Premium or G-Sync certification, only claiming Adaptive-Sync support from 48 Hz to 240 Hz. Innocn lists brightness for the QD-OLED panel at 250 nits typical and 400 nits peak/HDR. You still get two HDMI 2.1 ports and two DisplayPort 1.4 ports, along with a 3.5 mm headphone jack and two 3-watt speakers. The 2780S is now available at 20 percent off, ringing up at $279.99.

The Gigabyte GO27Q24G switches the formula up slightly, instead opting for a 27-inch Micro Lens Array+ White OLED (MLA+ OLED) display. As the counter to popular QD-OLED panels, WOLED panels tend to be brighter across the operating range. Indeed, Gigabyte states that the GO27Q24G offers a typical brightness of 275 nits and can hit a peak of 1,300 nits with HDR content.

The GO27Q24G is definitely a looker, as it sports a 4-sided borderless design with extremely thin bezels. It also features a RealBlack Glossy, which strikes a good balance between the color-enhancing clarity of a glossy screen coating and the reflection-rejecting characteristics of matte coatings.

The Gigabyte GO27Q24G is a 27-inch gaming monitor with a QHD resolution, 240 Hz refresh rate, and an MLA+ WOLED panel. It features a RealBlack Glossy display, a 4-sided borderless design, and can hit 1,300 nits peak brightness.

The monitor has AMD FreeSync Premium and Nvidia G-Sync Compatible certifications, along with a maximum refresh rate of 240 Hz. As for ports, you’ll get two HDMI 2.1, one DisplayPort 1.4, one USB-C, and one 3.5 mm headphone jack. However, one thing you won’t find is integrated speakers.

We recently reviewed the GO27Q24G and came away impressed with its value, video processing, and brightness. As our Christian Eberele explained, “Image and gaming experience are every bit the equal of monitors costing twice as much. The GO27Q24G’s Micro Lens Array delivers tremendous brightness, as much as 1,300 nits in HDR mode. It also has nearly perfect viewing angles. Color volume isn’t quite as large as panels with Quantum Dots or Tandem RGB Stripes, but you do get over 96% coverage of DCI-P3.”

The Gigabyte GO27Q24G is currently on sale for $297.47, representing a 15 percent discount.

The AOC Agon Pro AG276QZD2 is a 27-inch gaming monitor with a QHD resolution, 240 Hz refresh rate, and a QD-OLED panel. It supports AMD FreeSync Premium and Nvidia G-Sync and offers a peak brightness of 1,000 nits.

Finally, we have the AOC Agon Pro AG276QZD2, which is another 27-inch QD-OLED monitor that we reviewed nearly two years ago. The monitor supports AMD FreeSync Premium and Nvidia G-Sync, and has 250 nits typical brightness (1,000 nits peak). It also comes with two HDMI 2.0 ports, one DisplayPort 1.4 port, and a 3.5 mm headphone jack.

Eberle went on to write about the AG276QZD2, stating, “It has a huge color gamut thanks to Quantum Dot tech, which is larger than last year’s AG276QZD. Accuracy comes from a factory calibration that can only be slightly improved with adjustment. It also delivers the best gaming performance available from any monitor with perfect motion resolution and low input lag.”

If the AOC Agon Pro AG276QZD2 tickles your fancy, you can get it now for $297.49, which is a 15 percent discount.

#10346. Google Meet unlocks 1080p video streaming for all accounts on web

Streaming in 1080p will be the default in Google Meet, regardless of whether you pay for Google Workspace. That includes personal Google Accounts.

Google says users will default to sending and receiving 1080p video, at the minimum. Previously, Google Meet made 720p the standard for streaming, and a higher resolution had historically been locked behind the Google One 2TB plan. That’s been true since 1080p streaming was rolled out in 2023.

The upgrade will roll out to everyone, including those with Google Workspace accounts and personal Google Accounts. The only requirement to stream your own 1080p video in Google Meet for web will be hardware-based. Google’s press release states that devices with cameras capable of streaming in high definition will do so, and the feature will be turned on automatically.

It can be disabled, though. Users can head to Settings > Video in Google Meet for web. Video will also appear in a lower resolution if the user’s video tile is shrunk down on-screen.

The update appears to be centered on web-only instances of Google Meet, and might not include the app on Android or iOS. For the time being, those versions may still be limited to streaming in 720p for those who don’t have an active Google One subscription.

Google Meet started its extended rollout of 1080p streaming to everyone earlier this month, so it shouldn’t be much longer until the change has taken place completely.

The DRAM and NAND shortages have driven SSD prices through the roof, but we've still managed to track down some of the best Black Friday SSD deals around. #10341. Retail Merchandising Principles for Web Designers and Developers: Display Design, Ranking Rules and Measurement

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every e-commerce grid, product page and checkout screen is a shelf. Merchandisers have spent decades working out what to put at eye level, how many choices to show and how to tell whether a display works, and web teams are now rebuilding the same logic in code: sort orders, pinned products, badges, filters and A/B tests. Knowing where those ideas came from helps you build them with fewer mistakes.

This article maps physical retail merchandising onto digital product design. It covers visual hierarchy and choice load in listing UIs, a small rule-based ranking function you can test, accessible price and badge design with a contrast checker, and a significance test for layout experiments. It also covers why good plans fail at the handoff between design, engineering and operations. The numbers in the examples are illustrative, so use your own data. The code was run with Node.js 22.

Merchandising shapes how products are displayed, promoted and made accessible across sales channels. A well-planned strategy considers visual hierarchy, packaging, promotional materials, inventory timing and consistency between locations. For example, a skincare product may struggle to convert if testers are missing, shelf information is unclear or displays arrive after a campaign begins. If you build for the web, nearly every one of these problems has a direct counterpart:

Customers scan before they read. Nielsen Norman Group defines visual hierarchy as the arrangement of elements so the eye moves through them in order of importance, and names color and contrast, scale and grouping as the main tools. On a shelf, a larger facing or a contrasting background makes the hero product visible first. In a product grid, the equivalents are a larger card for a featured item, a clear boundary around a bundle, and a restrained use of badges so that only one or two items carry emphasis.

A common mistake is giving every card a badge, a color and a call to action. When everything is emphasized, nothing is, and the page reads like a crowded table in a store. Treat emphasis as a limited resource and have the design system say how many emphasized cards one screen may show.

A premium item placed beside entry-level alternatives may lose perceived value, and a crowded display can make fresh products look less appealing. Hick’s Law states that the time to make a decision increases with the number and complexity of choices, and its practical advice is to reduce options when speed matters and to highlight recommended ones. In a listing UI that suggests a curated first screen with a clear sort label, a few high-confidence recommendations and filters that handle the long tail, rather than an unbroken wall of products. Hick’s Law is a guide, not a rule, so treat it as a hypothesis to test on your own audience.

Merchandisers often ask for things like “pin the new range to the top, boost this collection, never show more than two items from one brand in a row, and push sold-out items down”. Implemented as scattered CSS order values and one-off queries, these requests become hard to audit. A small, pure function keeps the rules explicit and testable.

Because the function has no side effects, a short test can check every rule. This one confirms that pinned items lead, a pinned item that is out of stock is skipped, the brand cap pushes a third item from one brand behind other brands, sold-out items go last and a boost can lift a weaker product:

The design decision here is that stock demotion and the brand cap are defaults, while pins and boosts are inputs a merchandiser can change without a deploy, for example from a config file or admin screen. A real system would also log which rule moved each item, so that someone can answer why a product appears where it does.

Price cards and shelf labels are text, and text needs contrast. The WCAG contrast requirement asks for at least 4.5:1 for normal text and 3:1 for large text. Product cards are a place where this slips, because grey secondary prices and pastel sale badges look refined in a mockup. The following script computes the WCAG contrast ratio, so you can run it over your design tokens in CI.

It prints 4.54 for mid grey #767676 on white, which just passes for normal text, 7.00 for #595959, which would also meet the stricter enhanced level, and 3.96 for the pink #d94f70, which fails for small badge text but would pass for large text. A sale badge that fails the check should be fixed in the token, not on one page.

A useful display respects the realities of the sales environment: floor space, customer movement, staff workload, replenishment access and how much information shoppers can absorb at a glance. The digital version is the gap between the design file and what ships. Test the setup with the people who will use it, not only with the team that created it, because their feedback reveals practical problems before a wide rollout.

A polished plan can fail in practice when materials arrive late, displays are assembled incorrectly or teams miss replenishment. On the web, the matching failures are a promotion that goes live before its product data is synced, a badge that stays on after a sale ends, and a hero banner that links to a discontinued item. Execution needs coordination across marketing, suppliers, logistics and engineering, with clear instructions, realistic schedules, display standards and checks. Some companies outsource this kind of field merchandising. One provider’s services page, for example, lists planogram and retail resets, end-cap displays, signage placement, on-shelf availability, in-store audits and visit reporting. Whether or not you hire help, that list is a useful checklist of what execution involves, and most items have a digital twin you can automate: availability checks, expiry dates on promotions and audit reports. Treat any performance figures on vendor pages as the vendor’s own claims and ask for evidence before relying on them.

Timing matters most for seasonal promotions and limited windows. A campaign that goes live after peak demand has passed cannot deliver the same value, so give promotional content a start and end date in the data model and have a scheduled job remove it, rather than relying on someone to remember.

Sales alone cannot tell you whether visibility, placement, stockouts, confusing information or poor setup caused a result. For a web listing, the answer is a controlled experiment. The script below compares conversion for a control grid and a variant with a two-proportion z-test, a standard way to check whether a difference is larger than chance would explain.

With these illustrative numbers, conversion rises from 3.00 percent to 3.90 percent, a relative lift of 30.0 percent, and the z value of 2.21 exceeds the 1.96 threshold for roughly 95 percent confidence. That does not prove the curated grid is better in general. It says this difference is unlikely to be noise for this test, and it assumes you fixed the sample size in advance and did not stop the moment the result looked good. Use a proper experimentation platform or a statistician for anything with real revenue at stake.

A strong merchandising operation does not require a large budget. A simple guide for product priorities, layout, pricing, replenishment and approved materials keeps execution consistent, and it is the physical version of a design system: a small set of approved components and rules that make the correct result the easy path. For a product card, that means a documented component with a fixed set of badge types, a defined maximum of emphasized items per screen and tokens that already pass the contrast check.

Consistency also protects trust. Damaged fixtures, inconsistent packaging, missing prices and outdated signs make good products look poorly managed, and the digital equivalents are broken images, stale prices and mismatched labels between the listing and the product page. Aim for consistency, not identical pages: a mobile grid and a kiosk screen can differ, but they should share product priorities, visual identity and core messages.

Temporary and in-store selling spaces demand quick communication. Visitors move fast, attention is limited, and interfaces must be clear from a distance and forgiving of mistakes. If you build kiosk, tablet or point-of-sale software, these constraints shape the interface:

When results disappoint, audit the real experience before commissioning a redesign. Think like a first-time customer: is the offer clear, are premium items distinct and can the team maintain the page easily? Government small-business guidance offers concrete analogues. The Queensland Government’s retail display guidance recommends showing essential information and prices so customers can decide quickly, placing popular or profitable items at eye level, and refreshing window displays every two to four weeks. The Westminster City Council guide similarly links visual merchandising, layout and planned promotions to attracting customers and encouraging them to spend more time and money in a business.

Prioritize visibility, clarity, stock accuracy and consistent execution before investing in a full redesign. Small operational improvements can make a meaningful difference, especially when they remove obstacles that repeatedly interrupt the buying process.

The public guidance cited here was written for general small-business use, and UX principles such as visual hierarchy and Hick’s Law come from interface research, so they are useful analogies rather than guarantees. The code is a teaching sketch: it ignores personalization, pagination, caching, search relevance and many other concerns of a production ranking system. Results vary with category, audience and device, so test changes in your own setting and treat any benchmark as a starting point.

What is merchandising in e-commerce? It is how products are displayed, ordered, promoted and made accessible across a site or app, including layout, labels, badges, sort rules and inventory handling.

How does visual hierarchy apply to a product grid? The same tools apply: contrast, scale and grouping decide what the eye sees first and which products read as related.

What should I measure first? Start with conversion, add-to-cart rate and stock availability for the listing, then add average order value and revenue per visitor once the basics are tracked.

How do I know a layout test is reliable? Fix the sample size in advance, randomize users, run it long enough to cover weekly patterns and check significance, for example with the two-proportion test above.

Do small teams need a big design system? No. A short component guide with a few badge types, spacing rules and accessible color tokens is enough to keep pages consistent.

Merchandising is applied interface design with a long history. Clear hierarchy, a curated first screen, readable prices, explicit ranking rules and controlled experiments carry over directly from the shelf to the screen. For designers and developers, the practical lesson is that a good layout only counts if it ships correctly, stays current and is measured. Audit the basics, put the rules in code, test one change at a time and record what you learn.

#10340. Artex: The AI Pen-Test Tool Hackers Turned Into a Weapon

Forks of the open source Artex framework, along with 45 similar pen-testing tools, remain capable of executing credential attacks and cloud breaches at machine speed across multiple industries.

AI penetration-testing tool Artex has proven to be a boon for threat actors large and small, taking familiar attacker playbooks and deploying them at machine speeds — ushering in a potentially new era for defenders looking to get ahead of AI-enabled threats.

In July, independent developer Autumn-27 published Artex to GitHub, an open source, multiagent AI penetration testing framework written in Go. It is, by intention, a legitimate tool. However, as is so often the case with pen-test tools (such as, famously, Cobalt Strike), threat actors found a way to take advantage of it for malicious use. By late summer and early fall, a range of threat actors were using it as part of their toolkit.

Artex is capable of brute-forcing passwords, dumping databases, enumerating and abusing APIs, and more. This positions it for credential compromise and lateral movement in its own right, but the agentic element means Artex can operate at inhuman speeds. Microsoft saw an AI agent move from scanning GitHub for secrets to exfiltrating data from an Azure Key Vault in five minutes, for example.

Related:AI Agents Zip From Flaw Discovery to Root Access in 11 Minutes

Steve Winterfeld, advisory CISO for Akamai, tells Dark Reading that while legitimate tool misuse is a known quantity, AI reduces the effort and expertise required to execute complex attacks. "What is particularly noteworthy about the Artex campaigns is the acceleration in speed, coordination, and autonomy," he says. "Activities that previously required deliberate integration can now be chained together and executed with limited hacking skills."

At the end of October, Artex's creator deleted the original GitHub repository due to rampant abuse, but many forks of the tool remain, so the threat remains. But even if all the forks went offline and Artex was no longer a going concern, the underlying threat of frameworks like it is far from over.

GitGuardian yesterday, for instance, detailed 45 different agentic attack frameworks (Artex "siblings" as the researchers called them) that are all capable of conducting attacks autonomously with the help of an AI model. Depending on the tool, these pen-testing frameworks are capable of end-to-end attacks against a network, Web application targeting, and/or autonomous coding. Some even include built-in command-and-control functionality. That's not to say these tools are inherently bad — pen-testing tools have many legitimate use cases — but it does illustrate the wide range of options for today's attacker.

The best-known incident to date involved China's Storm-0940, which adapted Artex source code into its own offensive infrastructure. Last month, around Oct. 10, the actor used the Artex framework to launch a massive password-spraying and credential-stuffing campaign against more than 300 specific user accounts at eight critical organizations, making more than 151,000 authentication requests in the process. On average, this represented 2,900 attempts per minute without triggering automated lockout or alert.

Related:China's 'Armory Castle' Lays Siege to VMware vCenter Zero-Day

When this largely failed, Storm-0940 pivoted to target ConnectWise's internal infrastructure. Attackers exfiltrated trusted OAuth application credentials belonging to ConnectWise's Cloud Backup application, allowing them to compromise more than 2,250 downstream Microsoft 365 and Azure environments within three hours of initial targeting.

Abuse extends far beyond a single threat actor or objective. Microsoft last week published a blog post discussing how a financially motivated Cambodia-based threat cluster tracked as Storm-3358 started using Artex in its attacks against financial organizations primarily (but not exclusively) in Southeast Asia. Microsoft said it observed Storm-3358 using Artex in combination with conventional automated tools to scan systems connected to the public Internet, exploit vulnerabilities, and install open source remote access Trojans (RATs).

Related:Nok et norsk energiselskap har blitt utsatt for et datainnbrudd på to dager

Microsoft added that other threat actors have used Artex agents "to scan and exploit vulnerabilities in Web applications, use Web shells for persistence on compromised Web servers, compromise credentials from public sources and compromised systems, and use compromised cloud identities for persistence and data exfiltration."

CrowdStrike on Oct. 29 connected Artex-powered espionage activity to a threat actor it tracks as Vapor Panda, India's CERT (CERT-In) tracked a financially motivated threat actor hitting financial institutions, and as of Nov. 4, Censys observed more than 1,400 internet-exposed Artex console deployments. That said, because Internet exposure is not required for use, Censys estimated actual global use to be higher.

Recent Artex activity stands out because much of the story involves things security practitioners have seen time and time again. Pen-test tool abuse, nation-state tooling moving downstream to financially motivated attackers, password spraying, supply chain attacks, AI — it's a threat intelligence greatest hits album. But Guillaume Valadon, head of cybersecurity research at GitGuardian, tells Dark Reading what stands out to him most is the fast pace of attacks and the agent's initiative to accomplish the user's goals.

"At GitGuardian, we wanted to see how Artex handles secrets, so we ran it in a sealed lab," he says. "Artex ships no secret scanner, yet in every run where it acted, it found eight of the nine secrets. This included an AWS key recovered from git history. The model improvises on its hunt to reach the Artex user's goals."

Matt Snyder, Aviatrix head of security operations and threat research, says he was most surprised at how, despite the sophisticated nature of Artex's framework, the operational maturity of many threat actors deploying it appears to be low (given how many hosts remain Internet-exposed).

"AI is lowering the skill floor for offensive operations without improving operational discipline. The framework may be sophisticated, but the operational security surrounding many deployments is not," Snyder says. "This creates a dangerous combination in which more people can launch capable attacks, while poor security leaves their infrastructure, tooling, and potentially victim information exposed to anyone on the Internet."

Increasing sophistication for lower-grade attackers means, as always, defenders should be ready. Microsoft's Storm-3358 blog includes extensive indicators of compromise and recommends considering tools like defensive honeypots, which trick attackers into thinking they netted a valuable target.

Check Point advised in a blog post last week that organizations should expect greater automated pressure against exposed infrastructure. "Internet-facing systems should be continuously tested and hardened, with particular attention paid to weak authentication, exposed APIs, credential leakage, remote code execution (RCE) vulnerabilities and cloud storage permissions," the blog post read.

Censys principal security researcher Silas Cutler offered encouraging words. Because the underlying tradecraft is conventional, the fundamentals still hold, he said, "Enforce MFA, monitor your attack surface, and practice good credential hygiene."

Alex is an award-winning writer, journalist, and podcast host based in Boston. After cutting his teeth writing for independent gaming publications as a teenager, he graduated from Emerson College in 2016 with a Bachelor of Science in journalism. He has previously been published on VentureFizz, Search Security, Nintendo World Report, and elsewhere.

At Dark Reading, he covers a variety of cybersecurity topics, including the cybercrime ecosystem, open source security, and the intersection between AI and threat actors. In his spare time, Alex hosts the weekly Nintendo podcast, "Talk Nintendo Podcast," and works on personal writing projects, including two previously self-published science fiction novels.

He has received numerous awards, including TechTarget's Writer of the Year in 2022 as well as more than 10 Azbee awards for his reporting between 2022 and today.

Check the best prices on DDR5 and DDR4 memory kits.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In today's market, memory has become one of the most critical and expensive components in any build, and prices have only worsened thanks to the ongoing global DRAM shortage. We're keeping an eye on the volatile memory pricing to help identify any good deals that may arise.

Now, even more so than before the shortage began, price tags are fluctuating significantly from day to day, and it's becoming increasingly complex to tell when you're getting a fair deal versus overpaying for the same memory kit. We've compiled this comprehensive RAM price tracker that monitors the availability and pricing of different DDR5 and DDR4 memory kits across major U.S. retailers. Our goal is to take the guesswork out of memory shopping and help you make an informed purchasing decision.

We update this index regularly with the lowest prices available in the U.S. market for each memory kit. Our tracker covers a range of capacities and speeds across both budget and premium brands. We list the lowest price for each available memory kit, regardless of manufacturer, so you see the absolute best deals currently on offer.

The general availability of many memory kits is limited, with truly exceptional deals scarce and often selling out within seconds of appearing. High-capacity kits, particularly DDR5, frequently show the most severe stock shortages and price premiums. Therefore, timing and vigilance are especially important when shopping for memory in current times. Whether you're budget-conscious and want the best bang for your buck, or you're building a premium system and want the best RAM for gaming available, our index further below gives you the data you need to make the right purchase.

You can also check out our list of the best RAM and our RAM benchmark hierarchy to see evergreen performance data to help you make an informed decision when choosing a new GPU for your system.

Here are a few exceptional Black Friday RAM deals, but below that, we have a full list of all the lowest-priced RAM on the market.

Get a great deal on standalone RAM from Newegg. This deal is currently the lowest priced DDR5-6000 available. The T-Force Delta RGB not only adds flair to your chassis, but does so with decent speeds.

This RGB-laden kit from Corsair sports a CAS latency of 36 and is tested at 1.35 volts. This pair of 16GB memory sticks is colored black on the aluminum heat spreader, with RGB lighting running along the spine.

Above are a few of the standout deals from the Black Friday event, which is currently taking place. For a more extensive list of Black Friday deals, head to our Black Friday RAM deals article.

DDR5 memory prices have exploded over the last year, with triple and, in some cases, quadruple increases. For instance, a conventional 32GB (2x16GB) DDR5-6000 memory kit sold for around $100 to $200 in October 2025, but the same kit now starts at $430, if it's even in stock.

Retailers are taking advantage of the DRAM shortage, just as they did with the graphics card shortage a few years ago. They've started raising DDR5 prices to align with current market conditions and sometimes bundle DDR5 memory kits with processor or motherboard purchases.

Nonetheless, these DDR5 memory kits sometimes sell for more reasonable prices. However, you have to be quick to pull the trigger, as scalpers and automated shopping bots tend to gobble up what little stock is left.

The rising prices of DDR5 memory kits have forced some consumers to stick with DDR4. However, DDR4 has also seen price hikes over the last three months, though not as drastic as DDR5. We've seen the pricing double to triple.

For reference, it was common to find 32GB (2x16GB) DDR4 memory kits selling for between $60 to $90 back in October 2025. Jump to January 2026, and the same memory kit could cost anywhere between $150 and $180, so DDR4 isn't exactly a safe haven for consumers.

We've seen more reasonably priced DDR4 memory kits on the market than DDR5. Logically, it makes little sense to build a DDR4-based system now due to processor and motherboard availability. However, if you're in desperate need of a DDR4 memory upgrade, there are viable options on the market.

Say hello to a new 3D printer and goodbye to wasteful filament “poop.”

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Tool changers — 3D printers with multiple heads — have been a major focus of the industry for more than a year, and for good reason. Multiple heads mean you don’t have to purge the nozzle every time your print swaps colors or materials, which has the dual benefit of making complex prints much faster while saving a massive amount of filament waste or “poop,” which is a problem with more traditional multi-color AMS, single-toolhead printers.

But while these tool changers typically cost multiple thousands of dollars a few years ago, they’ve gotten much more affordable in the past year, starting with Snapmaker’s sub-$1,000 U1 (now just $699) and then Fashforge’s Creator 5 not long after. And both of those printers, plus a traditional four-color AMS printer designed to minimize wasted filament, are all on sale at their lowest-ever prices as we head into Black Friday.So if you’re thinking of diving into the 3D printing realm or your existing printer is getting long in the tooth, now is a great time to make the leap to a tool changer and see how it can change your printing game — or perhaps your printing business.

Snapmaker’s U1 kicked off the affordable tool changer craze around this time last year, with an early Kickstarter price of $799 and a full retail price of $999. But now it’s widely available and on sale for $100 less than ever. When we reviewed the U1, we praised its roomy build area (270 mm cubed), direct-drive tool heads, and its four-head tool-changing capabilities, earning it 4.5 stars and a spot on our best 3D printers list.

Last year, this tool-changer was the most anticipated upcoming printer around. With four heads for swapping colors or materials without the need to "poop," it can breeze through complex multicolor prints much faster than single-head printers, without wasting nearly as much filament.

On the heels of the U1, Flashforge released the Creator 5, a tool changer that’s a bit more basic, but still a solid machine (we gave it 4 stars in our review), priced even lower than the Snapmaker, both when it launched (at $699) and now, when it’s on sale for $100 less. You can grab that base model, or the Creator 5 Pro, which adds an enclosure with a cooling fan and filtration for more consistent temperatures, which can prevent warping and generally lead to better prints.

This open CoreXY 3D printer has four toolheads, is rated for head swaps that take 7 seconds, and sports a 256 mm X3 build volume.

An upgraded version of the Creator 5, you get the same 4 tool heads and build volume, now with an enclosed chamber with a fan and filter. If you're sensitive to PLA or PETG fumes, your workspace lacks ventilation (or is drafty), or you print with more exotic filaments, it's worth paying extra for.

Last up, and least expensive on this list, is Anycubic’s Kobra X. This isn’t a tool changer, as it has a single head. Instead, it’s a more traditional four-color printer that aims to reduce filament waste by putting the filament cutter much closer to the nozzle tip. It also takes up less space, as it's designed to keep four filament spools on a rack up top rather than a separate AMD.I’ve owned this printer for going on a year, and you’ll still see a lot of filament waste when tackling multi-color prints, but it does create less poop than previous printers I’ve owned. And if you can’t afford to spend more than $500, the Kobra X delivers solid multi-color printing at a price that’s tough to beat. It’s currently on sale for its lowest-ever price of just $279.

This low-priced, space-saving four-color 3D printer has a 260 mm X3 build volume, houses four filament spools up top, and is quick when swapping filaments. It also saves filament waste by cutting closer to the nozzle tip when switching, but it will still "poop," and can waste a lot of filament if you regularly tackle multi-color prints.

#10331. Nothing Ear (note) is yet another AI recorder, but with earbuds inside

Set to launch on December 8, Nothing has confirmed its mysterious AI product will be called “Ear (note),” and it’s an AI recorder with earbuds inside.

Nothing is entering a crowded market here with products from Plaud, Amazon, and countless others already offering AI recording/transcribing products, but building in earbuds is indeed a unique selling point.

Nothing Ear (note) is launching on December 8, though pricing is unclear. Pei’s comments suggest a subscription will be used in some capacity.
